<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove sharp contraction of the information carried by a binary channel under independent symmetric noise on a uniform discrete cube. At fixed initial information, a noisy coordinate retains the most information. The Boolean specialization resolves the Courtade–Kumar conjecture and gives an output-entropy refinement. We also establish a stronger mean-dependent entropy-production bound. The proof combines an explicit three-point optimizer for the local joining problem, two entropy capacities, and a common-output thinning inequality, followed by dimension induction and integration along the noise semigroup.

- **claim-soft-contraction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-main-soft)): For every finite $n\ge0$, every $u:\Omega_n\to[-1,1]$, and $-1\le\rho\le1$, $$I(T_\rho u) \le\psi\!\left(\|\rho\|\,\psi^{-1}(I(u))\right). \label{main:interpolation}$$ The inverse is the nonnegative increasing branch on $[0,L]$. For $n\ge1$, every soft coordinate $u(x)=a x_i$, $\|a\|\le1$, attains equality.
  - Explanation: The information of a noisy soft binary channel is at most that of a noisy coordinate with the same initial information; soft coordinates attain equality.
- **claim-boolean-ck-bound** (theorem, [source](#stmt-main-ck)): For every finite $n\ge0$, $\rho\in[-1,1]$, and $f:\Omega_n\to\{-1,1\}$, put $m=\mathbb Ef$ and $r_m=\psi^{-1}(H(m))$. Then $$I(f(X);Y)\le\psi(\|\rho\|r_m)\le\psi(\|\rho\|). \label{main:bound}$$ For $n\ge1$, signed coordinate functions attain the bound at $m=0$. If $0<\|m\|<1$ and $0<\|\rho\|\le1$, the refined upper curve is strictly below the universal curve $\psi(\|\rho\|)$.
  - Explanation: For a Boolean summary the information is bounded by a curve that depends on the output entropy, which lies below the universal bound; this gives the Courtade–Kumar inequality.
- **claim-sharp-production** (theorem, [source](#stmt-upgrade-production)): For every finite $n\ge0$ and every $g:\{-1,1\}^n\to(-1,1)$, with $m=\mathbb Eg$, $$D(g)\ge\widehat{\mathcal B}_m(I(g)) =\max\{\mathcal B_m(I(g)),F(I(g))\} \ge F(I(g)). \label{upgrade:production-bound}$$
  - Explanation: The entropy production of an interior soft field is bounded below by a mean-dependent profile, which dominates the coordinate rate.
- **claim-entropy-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-scalar-entropy-bounds)): For $\|r\|\le1$, $$\label{scalar:entropy-quadratic-bounds} \frac{r^2}{2}\le\psi(r)\le Lr^2,\qquad H(r)\ge L(1-r^2).$$ The quotient $\psi(r)/r^2$ is strictly increasing for $0<r<1$. Moreover, $$\label{scalar:logarithm-constants} \frac23<L<\frac{25}{36}<\frac7{10},\qquad \frac12<M:=\frac{4L^2}{3}<\frac23.$$
  - Explanation: Quadratic bounds for the entropy gap and binary entropy, monotonicity of a quotient, and rational bounds on the logarithm constant.
- **claim-rate-convexity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-scalar-profile-convexity)): The function $F$ is smooth on $[0,L)$, with right derivatives at zero, and $$\label{scalar:production-convexity} F(0)=0,\qquad F'(0)=2,\qquad F''(0)=\frac43,\qquad F''(I)\ge\frac43,\qquad F'''(I)\ge0.$$ The zero-extended surplus $S$ is nonnegative, nondecreasing, convex, and $C^1$ on $(-\infty,L)$, with $S(0)=S'(0)=0$. It is not $C^2$ at zero.
  - Explanation: Smoothness and convexity properties of the coordinate rate and its surplus over the linear rate.
- **claim-curvature-growth** (theorem, [source](#stmt-scalar-curvature-growth)): The function $I\mapsto e^{-2I}F''(I)$ is strictly increasing on $[0,L)$. For $I>0$ it satisfies $$\frac{F'''(I)}{F''(I)}>2.$$
  - Explanation: The curvature of the rate grows faster than an exponential factor.
- **claim-scaled-curvature** (bound, [source](#stmt-scalar-scaled-curvature)): For $0<h\le L$, put $$\label{scalar:scaled-curvature-definition} P(h)=h^2F''(L-h).$$ Then $$\label{scalar:scaled-curvature-range} \frac12\le P(h)\le M=\frac{4L^2}{3},\qquad P(L)=M.$$
  - Explanation: The entropy-scaled curvature of the rate stays between two explicit constants.
- **claim-priced-core** (theorem, [source](#stmt-canonical-priced)): For every $p\ge0$, the minimum of $$\mathbb E\{c(A,B)+p[H(A)+H(B)]\}, \qquad \mathbb EA=a,\quad \mathbb EB=b,$$ has a unique optimizer. It is one ordered interior pair $(x,y)$ and possible equal corners, as in <a href="#canonical:law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:law">[canonical:law]</a>. Its core is given by the unique minimizer of $F_p$ subject to <a href="#canonical:floors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:floors">[canonical:floors]</a>, using <a href="#canonical:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:coordinates">[canonical:coordinates]</a>; its masses are <a href="#canonical:masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:masses">[canonical:masses]</a>. At $p=0$, the optimizer is the deterministic pair $(a,b)$.
  - Explanation: For every nonnegative entropy price the priced joining problem has a unique optimizer made of one interior pair and possible equal corners.
- **claim-entropy-coverage** (theorem, [source](#stmt-canonical-coverage)): For every $0<e\le e_{\max}$, a law of the form <a href="#canonical:law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:law">[canonical:law]</a> attains <a href="#canonical:budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:budget">[canonical:budget]</a> and has average entropy exactly $e$.
  - Explanation: Every admissible average entropy is attained exactly by a three-point optimizer of the budgeted joining problem.
- **claim-onebit-capacity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-capacity-onebit)): Let $g(X)=m+\delta X$, where $X$ is a uniform sign and $\|m\|+\|\delta\|<1$. Write $\alpha=1-m^2$ and $$J=\frac{\psi(m+\delta)+\psi(m-\delta)}2-\psi(m), \qquad D=\frac\delta2\{V(m+\delta)-V(m-\delta)\}.$$ Then $J<\alpha L$ and $$D\ge\alpha F(J/\alpha). \label{capacity:refined}$$
  - Explanation: A variance-refined one-bit inequality bounds the production from below by the rate of the rescaled information.
- **claim-corner-geometry** (theorem, [source](#stmt-reserve-geometry)): For $-1<m<1$ and $0\le\delta<1-\|m\|$, put $$A=1-m,\qquad \kappa=\frac12\log\frac1{1-\delta^2/(1+m)^2}.$$ Then $$A^2\log\frac\alpha\beta\le3\kappa. \label{reserve:log-geometry}$$
  - Explanation: A logarithmic inequality controlling the ratio of variance capacities by a relative-entropy parameter.
- **claim-common-output-contraction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-reserve-corner)): For every physical state $(m,\delta,e)$ and $0<\tau\le1$, $$W(1-\tau+\tau m,\tau\delta,\tau e) \le\tau W(m,\delta,e). \label{reserve:plus-corner}$$ The same inequality holds with parent mean $-1+\tau+\tau m$, corresponding to the negative common output. There is no sign restriction on the original mean.
  - Explanation: The required payment scales sublinearly when a common output is added, for either sign of the output.
- **claim-reserve-joining** (theorem, [source](#stmt-reserve-joining)): Let $(A,B)$ have any joint law on $[-1,1]^2$ with $e_a=\mathbb EH(A)>0$ and $e_b=\mathbb EH(B)>0$. Put $$a=\mathbb EA,\quad b=\mathbb EB,\quad m=\frac{a+b}{2}, \quad e=\frac{e_a+e_b}{2},$$ and $I=H(m)-e$, $I_a=H(a)-e_a$, $I_b=H(b)-e_b$. Then $$\mathbb Ec(A,B)\ge \mathcal B_m(I)-\frac{\mathcal B_a(I_a)+\mathcal B_b(I_b)}2. \label{reserve:local-bound}$$
  - Explanation: The expected joining cost of any pair law pays the increment of the reserve profile between parent and children.
- **claim-common-output-payment** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thinning-payment)): For the core and mass in <a href="#thinning:path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:path">[thinning:path]</a>, $$\label{thinning:value} F(\nu+J)-F(\nu)\le\lambda F(J_c).$$
  - Explanation: Along the common-output path the rate increment is at most the core mass times the rate of the core information gap.
- **claim-interval-estimate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thinning-interval)): Let $0\le x<1$, and put $$p=1+x,\qquad \sigma=1-x,\qquad \gamma=\frac{\sigma}{p},\qquad \beta=\frac{\log(1+x)}L.$$ Suppose $$0\le z<\gamma,\qquad \sigma-pz\le u\le1-z.$$ For $\beta>0$, define $k_\beta(v)=(v-\beta)_+/v^2$ for $v>0$ and $k_\beta(0)=0$. For $\beta=0$, set $k_0(v)=1/v$ for $v>0$. Then $$\label{thinning:interval-bound} \int_u^{u+z}k_\beta(v)\,dv \le-\frac32\log(1-\gamma z).$$ If $x>0$, the endpoint $z=\gamma$ is also allowed. At $z=0$, both sides are zero.
  - Explanation: An integral of a cutoff kernel over admissible entropy intervals is bounded by a logarithmic expression.
- **claim-entropy-window** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thinning-window)): Every state on <a href="#thinning:path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:path">[thinning:path]</a> satisfies $$\label{thinning:window-bound} \int_q^s\frac{(h-b)_+}{h^2}\,dh\le3\kappa.$$
  - Explanation: On every thinning path the entropy-interval integral is at most a fixed multiple of the relative-entropy parameter.
- **claim-high-entropy-value** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thinning-high-value)): Let $x\ge0$, $d>0$, and $x+d<1$. Define $$\bar h(x,d)=\frac{H(x+d)+H(x-d)}2,\qquad e_0(x,d)=(1-x)H\!\left(\frac d{1-x}\right).$$ For every $e_0(x,d)\le e\le\bar h(x,d)$, $$\label{thinning:high-value-bound} \mathcal K(x,d,e)\ge J_0(x,d,e):=F(H(x)-e)-F(\bar h(x,d)-e).$$
  - Explanation: On the closed high-entropy branch the canonical joining cost dominates the rate increment.
- **claim-high-information-reserve** (theorem, [source](#stmt-upgrade-high-information)): For $\|m\|<1$ and $L/2\le I<H(m)$, $$\mathcal B_m(I)\ge F(I). \label{upgrade:high-information-bound}$$ The inequality is strict if $m\ne0$. Consequently a state with $F(I)>\mathcal B_m(I)$ must have $I<L/2$.
  - Explanation: Above half information the reserve profile dominates the coordinate rate, strictly at nonzero mean.
- **claim-wall-monotonicity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-wall-monotonicity)): Let $m,d>0$, $m+d<1$, and $$0<e\le\bar h(m,d):=\frac{H(m+d)+H(m-d)}2.$$ Put $I=H(m)-e$, $j=\bar h(m,d)-e$, and $J=I-j$. If $I\le L/2$, then at fixed $d,e$, $$\partial_m\{F(I)-F(j)\}>0. \label{wall:positive-derivative}$$ At $j=0$, the derivative is interpreted from the physical side.
  - Explanation: Below half information the demand increment strictly increases with the common mean at fixed separation and entropy.
- **claim-strengthened-joining** (theorem, [source](#stmt-upgrade-joining)): Let $(A,B)$ have a joint law on $[-1,1]^2$ with $e_a=\mathbb EH(A)>0$ and $e_b=\mathbb EH(B)>0$. Put $$a=\mathbb EA,\quad b=\mathbb EB,\quad m=\frac{a+b}{2},\quad e=\frac{e_a+e_b}{2},\quad I=H(m)-e,$$ and $I_a=H(a)-e_a$, $I_b=H(b)-e_b$. Then $$\mathbb Ec(A,B)\ge \widehat{\mathcal B}_m(I) -\frac{\widehat{\mathcal B}_a(I_a) +\widehat{\mathcal B}_b(I_b)}2. \label{upgrade:local-inequality}$$
  - Explanation: The joining cost pays the increment of the maximum profile between parent and children for any pair law.

      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **uniform cube** ([source](#main:section)): Let $\Omega_n=\{-1,1\}^n$ carry uniform measure.
- **information of a soft channel** ([source](#main:section)): For a soft binary channel $u:\Omega_n\to[-1,1]$, define $$I(u)=H(\mathbb Eu)-\mathbb EH(u)=\mathbb E\psi(u)-\psi(\mathbb Eu).$$
- **noise operator** ([source](#main:section)): The noise operator is $T_\rho u(y)=\mathbb E[u(X)\mid Y=y]$.
- **inverse hyperbolic tangent** ([source](#scalar:surplus-definition)): Write $V(r)=\operatorname{atanh}r$ for $-1<r<1$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-entropy-bounds** [claim](#stmt-scalar-entropy-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-scalar-entropy-bounds): Series expansion of the entropy gap and comparison of coefficients. -> quadratic entropy bounds and logarithm constants
- **step-rate-convexity** [claim](#stmt-scalar-profile-convexity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-scalar-profile-convexity): Hyperbolic parametrization and a positive integral for the second derivative. -> convexity of rate and surplus
- **step-curvature-growth** [claim](#stmt-scalar-curvature-growth) / [proof](#proof-stmt-scalar-curvature-growth): Logarithmic derivative of the curvature in the hyperbolic parameter. -> curvature growth
- **step-scaled-curvature** [claim](#stmt-scalar-scaled-curvature) / [proof](#proof-stmt-scalar-scaled-curvature): Moment integrals, Jensen, Cauchy–Schwarz and covariance monotonicity. -> two-sided bound on scaled curvature
- **step-priced-core** [claim](#stmt-canonical-priced) / [proof](#proof-stmt-canonical-priced): Strict convexity of a priced objective and a global affine support inequality. -> unique three-point optimizer
- **step-entropy-coverage** [claim](#stmt-canonical-coverage) / [proof](#proof-stmt-canonical-coverage): Continuity of the optimizer entropy in the price and a limit at large price. -> optimizer with prescribed entropy
- **step-onebit-capacity** [claim](#stmt-capacity-onebit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-capacity-onebit): Posterior representations, size-biased Jensen inequality for the secant slope. -> variance-refined one-bit inequality
- **step-corner-geometry** [claim](#stmt-reserve-geometry) / [proof](#proof-stmt-reserve-geometry): Monotonicity of a logarithmic ratio and an auxiliary function. -> logarithmic corner inequality
- **step-corner-contraction** [claim](#stmt-reserve-corner) / [proof](#proof-stmt-reserve-corner): Euler-type derivative along the common-output ray with the intercept bound. -> sublinear scaling of the payment
- **step-reserve-joining** [claim](#stmt-reserve-joining) / [proof](#proof-stmt-reserve-joining): Pay the deterministic core, then add the two common corners, then pool children. -> local joining for the reserve profile
- **step-interval-estimate** [claim](#stmt-thinning-interval) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thinning-interval): Affine family of intervals, bound of the derivative and a polynomial inequality. -> integral estimate for the cutoff kernel
- **step-entropy-window** [claim](#stmt-thinning-window) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thinning-window): Entropy floor of the oriented core, enlargement of the interval, application of the interval estimate. -> window integral bound
- **step-common-output-payment** [claim](#stmt-thinning-payment) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thinning-window-2): Monotonicity of the normalized increment in the core mass. -> payment inequality
- **step-high-entropy-value** [claim](#stmt-thinning-high-value) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thinning-high-value): Represent the branch by a thinning path and combine the one-bit bound with the payment. -> cost dominates rate increment
- **step-high-information** [claim](#stmt-upgrade-high-information) / [proof](#proof-stmt-upgrade-high-information): Exponential balance of the centered curvature integral and tangent inequality for the surplus. -> reserve dominates rate above half information
- **step-wall-monotonicity** [claim](#stmt-wall-monotonicity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-wall-monotonicity): Compare rate slopes with entropy slopes through two logarithmic ratio bounds. -> monotone demand increment
- **step-strengthened-joining** [claim](#stmt-upgrade-joining) / [proof](#proof-stmt-upgrade-joining): Split by the active parent profile; pool with the rate; move the common mean to the one-corner wall. -> local joining for the maximum profile
- **step-sharp-production** [claim](#stmt-upgrade-production) / [proof](#proof-stmt-upgrade-joining-2): Dimension induction with the exact energy decomposition. -> sharp production bound
- **step-soft-contraction** [claim](#stmt-main-soft) / [proof](#proof-stmt-upgrade-joining-3): Integrate the production bound in the inverse-entropy coordinate along the noise semigroup. -> sharp soft-channel contraction
- **step-boolean-ck-bound** [claim](#stmt-main-ck) / [proof](#proof-stmt-upgrade-joining-4): Specialize to Boolean fields, where the conditional entropy vanishes. -> Boolean bound with output bias

          "exact_quote": "For every finite $n\\ge0$, every $u:\\Omega_n\\to[-1,1]$, and $-1\\le\\rho\\le1$, $$I(T_\\rho u)\n \\le\\psi\\!\\left(|\\rho|\\,\\psi^{-1}(I(u))\\right).\n \\label{main:interpolation}$$ The inverse is the nonnegative increasing branch on $[0,L]$.",
          "source_id": "PM-119.1"
        }
      ],

        "sharp production bound"
      ],

      "output": "sharp soft-channel contraction",

    },

    }

}

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-sharp-soft-contraction**: Among all soft binary channels with the same initial information, a soft coordinate retains the most information after independent symmetric noise, and the information after noise is bounded by the coordinate curve evaluated at the contracted effective amplitude.
- **inv-boolean-output-bias-refinement**: For a Boolean summary, the information about the noisy observation is bounded by a curve that depends on the actual entropy of the Boolean output, and this curve lies below the universal curve whenever the output is biased and the noise is present.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-nonuniform-source**: Apply the coordinate-optimality bound to inputs whose bits are biased or correlated. -> The bound is proved only for the uniform product measure; for other sources the coordinate need not be optimal and the bound need not hold.
- **anti-biased-source-attains-bound**: Claim that every biased Boolean summary attains the refined upper curve. -> The refined curve is only an upper bound; for biased outputs it lies strictly below the universal curve and attainment is not asserted.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The results are analytic inequalities over real-valued channels and transcendental entropy functions, proved by calculus, convexity, dimension induction and integration along the noise semigroup. They state no finite procedure with explicit steps whose output a finite exact computation could certify; the optimizer is characterised by real-variable minimisation, so no exact finite oracle decides any claim.

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The contraction bound is a checkable inequality: a library can compute the information of a soft channel on a small cube and compare it with the sharp bound after noise. This is a test oracle for numerical code, not an algorithm the paper gives. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No simulation, game mechanic or stochastic process is built; the paper proves a static information inequality on the uniform cube. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | A property-based testing tool can use the sharp bound and the coordinate equality case as an invariant when validating entropy or mutual-information routines on small cubes. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper contains no algorithm with steps or complexity; the optimizer for the local joining problem is used only inside the proof to obtain a lower bound on cost. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No data structure, representation or query interface is introduced; the objects are real-valued fields on the cube and scalar profiles. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the result concerns circuits, parallel evaluation or arithmetic units; the statements are analytic inequalities. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem says a single coordinate retains the most information under noise, so it gives no diffusion, hardness or security guarantee and says nothing about adversaries. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No learning procedure, generalization statement or model class appears; the functions are arbitrary soft or Boolean fields on the cube and the result is a fixed-noise information bound. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The constrained minimization of a joining cost is a proof device with a closed-form three-point solution, not a modelled decision problem, and the paper does not claim it as an optimisation method. |
| distributed_systems | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | Where independent fair bits are observed through independent symmetric noisy links and only one bit may be forwarded, the corollary says forwarding one signed coordinate maximizes the information retained. This is a design heuristic at most. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The natural home of this result is information theory and analysis on the cube, which is pure mathematics. No further engineering domain is claimed beyond the ones judged above. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-uniform-cube** (hypothesis): Every result is proved for the uniform product measure on the discrete cube. Nothing is claimed for biased or correlated input bits. Blocked use: ['Applying the coordinate-optimality bound to sources whose bits are biased or correlated', 'Quoting the information curves for non-uniform inputs']
- **lim-one-bit-summary** (hypothesis): The comparison concerns one-bit summaries, that is soft binary channels or Boolean functions. It gives no statement about summaries with several output bits. Blocked use: ['Using single-coordinate optimality to justify multi-bit digests or sketches', 'Extending the bound to vector-valued outputs']
- **lim-finite-dimension** (edge_case): The contraction statements are stated for every finite dimension. The proofs use dimension induction and finite-dimensional differentiation, so no infinite-dimensional or limiting statement is asserted. Blocked use: ['Taking a dimension-free limit of the bound without a separate argument', 'Applying the result to non-finite product spaces']
- **lim-attainment-only-coordinates** (counterexample_scope): Attainment is stated only for signed coordinate functions at zero output mean. The refined curve for biased Boolean outputs is an upper bound and its attainment is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Claiming that a biased Boolean source attains the refined upper curve', 'Using the refined curve as an equality for non-coordinate functions']
- **lim-interior-production** (hypothesis): The sharp production bound is stated for fields with values strictly inside the open unit interval. Boundary-valued inputs are handled separately in the integration argument and not by this statement. Blocked use: ['Applying the production bound directly to fields that take the endpoint values', 'Evaluating the logarithmic slope function at a boundary value']
- **lim-positive-child-entropy** (hypothesis): The local joining inequalities require both child average entropies to be positive. With zero entropy the child means can reach the boundary and the cost can be infinite, so the statement is not claimed there. Blocked use: ['Using the joining bound for pair laws with a degenerate child', 'Dropping the positivity condition when inducting on dimension']
- **lim-oriented-path-entropy-floor** (formal_scope): The lower entropy bound used in the common-output estimate holds along the oriented path with a nonnegative core center. It is not a bound for every pair law having the same means. Blocked use: ['Applying the entropy floor to arbitrary pair laws', 'Using the window estimate without the nonnegative-center orientation']
- **lim-entropy-price-nonconstructive** (nonconstructive): The price that realises a prescribed average entropy is obtained from continuity and a limit at large price, not computed. The three-point optimizer is therefore characterised, not produced by a finite procedure with a complexity guarantee. Blocked use: ['Presenting the optimizer as an algorithm with proven running time', 'Certifying an exact price by a finite computation from this argument alone']
- **lim-profile-comparison-scope** (formal_scope): The comparison with the other production profile is an asymptotic statement about two explicit profiles, as the entropy deficit tends to zero. It is not a comparison of the resulting contraction theorems. Blocked use: ['Concluding that the mean-dependent profile yields a stronger contraction theorem', 'Extrapolating the asymptotic ratio to moderate entropy deficit']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 119 (`lean/docs/119.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Sharp binary-information contraction on the discrete cube](../../preprints/Sharp-binary-information-contraction-on-the-discrete-cube-September-24-2026/main.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves sharp contraction of the information carried by a binary channel under independent symmetric noise on a uniform discrete cube. At each fixed initial information level, a noisy coordinate channel attains the maximum retained information. It also proves the refined Boolean bound that accounts for output bias, with strict improvement for nonconstant biased outputs at nonzero noise correlation, and the selected mean-dependent entropy-production inequality.
>
> For Boolean functions this includes the Courtade–Kumar inequality $I(f(X);Y)\le1-h_2(\varepsilon)$ in bits at crossover probability $0\le\varepsilon\le1/2$, where $h_2$ is binary entropy. A coordinate and its complement attain equality.

Comparator source `SoftChannel204.lean` (4486 bytes, sha256 `31e3e3fc69bd044ec8543cacd6035887d5bf6670c94b31462bb8094f6acfd226`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section

open scoped BigOperators

namespace SoftChannel204

abbrev Cube (n : ℕ) := Fin n → Bool

def sign (b : Bool) : ℝ := if b then 1 else -1

def avg {n : ℕ} (g : Cube n → ℝ) : ℝ := (∑ x, g x) / (2 : ℝ) ^ n

def L : ℝ := Real.log 2

def psi (r : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  (1 + r) / 2 * Real.log (1 + r) + (1 - r) / 2 * Real.log (1 - r)

def H (r : ℝ) : ℝ := L - psi r

def info {n : ℕ} (u : Cube n → ℝ) : ℝ := H (avg u) - avg (H ∘ u)

def psiInv (s : ℝ) : ℝ := Function.invFunOn psi (Set.Icc 0 1) s

def kernel {n : ℕ} (ρ : ℝ) (x y : Cube n) : ℝ :=
  ∏ i, if x i = y i then (1 + ρ) / 2 else (1 - ρ) / 2

def noise {n : ℕ} (ρ : ℝ) (u : Cube n → ℝ) (y : Cube n) : ℝ :=
  ∑ x, kernel ρ y x * u x

def Closed {n : ℕ} (u : Cube n → ℝ) : Prop := ∀ x, u x ∈ Set.Icc (-1) 1

def Interior {n : ℕ} (g : Cube n → ℝ) : Prop := ∀ x, g x ∈ Set.Ioo (-1) 1

def softCoordinate {n : ℕ} (i : Fin n) (a : ℝ) (x : Cube n) : ℝ := a * sign (x i)

def flip {n : ℕ} (i : Fin n) (x : Cube n) : Cube n := Function.update x i (!(x i))

def numberOperator {n : ℕ} (g : Cube n → ℝ) (x : Cube n) : ℝ :=
  (1 / 2 : ℝ) * ∑ i, (g x - g (flip i x))

def production {n : ℕ} (g : Cube n → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  avg (fun x => Real.artanh (g x) * numberOperator g x)

/-- Defined by this formula on `[0, L)`; no finite endpoint value at `L` is asserted. -/
def F (s : ℝ) : ℝ := psiInv s * Real.artanh (psiInv s)

/-- The excess `F s - 2 * s`, used for `s < L`, is extended by zero to negative arguments. -/
def S (s : ℝ) : ℝ := if s < 0 then 0 else F s - 2 * s

/-- The reserve is used for `|m| < 1` and `0 ≤ I < H m`. -/
def reserve (m I : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  2 * I + (1 - m ^ 2) * S (L - (H m - I) / (1 - m ^ 2))

def hybrid (m I : ℝ) : ℝ := max (reserve m I) (F I)

def softJoint {n : ℕ} (ρ : ℝ) (u : Cube n → ℝ) (b : Bool) (y : Cube n) : ℝ :=
  avg (fun x => (1 + sign b * u x) / 2 * kernel ρ x y)

def boolJoint {n : ℕ} (ρ : ℝ) (f : Cube n → Bool) (b : Bool) (y : Cube n) : ℝ :=
  avg (fun x => if f x = b then kernel ρ x y else 0)

def leftMarginal {n : ℕ} (p : Bool → Cube n → ℝ) (b : Bool) : ℝ := ∑ y, p b y

def rightMarginal {n : ℕ} (p : Bool → Cube n → ℝ) (y : Cube n) : ℝ := ∑ b, p b y

def mutualInformation {n : ℕ} (p : Bool → Cube n → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  ∑ b, ∑ y, if 0 < p b y then
    p b y * Real.log (p b y / (leftMarginal p b * rightMarginal p y)) else 0

def binaryEntropyBits (ε : ℝ) : ℝ :=
  -(ε * Real.log ε + (1 - ε) * Real.log (1 - ε)) / L

def SoftContraction : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (u : Cube n → ℝ), Closed u →
  ∀ ρ ∈ Set.Icc (-1 : ℝ) 1,
    info (noise ρ u) ≤ psi (|ρ| * psiInv (info u))

def SoftAttainment : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (i : Fin n) (a : ℝ), |a| ≤ 1 →
  ∀ ρ ∈ Set.Icc (-1 : ℝ) 1,
    info (noise ρ (softCoordinate i a)) =
      psi (|ρ| * psiInv (info (softCoordinate i a)))

def SoftInformationIdentity : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (u : Cube n → ℝ), Closed u →
  ∀ ρ ∈ Set.Icc (-1 : ℝ) 1,
    mutualInformation (softJoint ρ u) = info (noise ρ u)

def RefinedBoolean : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (f : Cube n → Bool)
  (ρ : ℝ), ρ ∈ Set.Icc (-1 : ℝ) 1 →
  let m := avg (sign ∘ f)
  let bound := psi (|ρ| * psiInv (H m))
  mutualInformation (boolJoint ρ f) = info (noise ρ (sign ∘ f)) ∧
  mutualInformation (boolJoint ρ f) ≤ bound ∧ bound ≤ psi |ρ| ∧
  (0 < |m| → |m| < 1 → 0 < |ρ| → bound < psi |ρ|)

def BooleanAttainment : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (i : Fin n)
  (ρ : ℝ), ρ ∈ Set.Icc (-1 : ℝ) 1 →
  mutualInformation (boolJoint ρ (fun x => x i)) = psi |ρ| ∧
  mutualInformation (boolJoint ρ (fun x => !(x i))) = psi |ρ|

def BitsConversion : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (f : Cube n → Bool)
  (ε : ℝ), ε ∈ Set.Icc (0 : ℝ) (1/2) →
  psi (1 - 2 * ε) / L = 1 - binaryEntropyBits ε ∧
  mutualInformation (boolJoint (1 - 2 * ε) f) / L ≤ 1 - binaryEntropyBits ε

def SharpProduction : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (g : Cube n → ℝ), Interior g →
  hybrid (avg g) (info g) ≤ production g ∧ F (info g) ≤ hybrid (avg g) (info g)

def FullMain : Prop := SoftContraction ∧ SoftAttainment ∧ SoftInformationIdentity ∧
  RefinedBoolean ∧ BooleanAttainment ∧ BitsConversion ∧ SharpProduction

theorem fullMain : FullMain := by
  sorry

end SoftChannel204

end

end OAI
```

      "statement_lean": "def softJoint {n : ℕ} (ρ : ℝ) (u : Cube n → ℝ) (b : Bool) (y : Cube n) : ℝ :=\n  avg (fun x => (1 + sign b * u x) / 2 * kernel ρ x y)"

    },

      "docstring": null,

      "statement_lean": "def SoftInformationIdentity : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (u : Cube n → ℝ), Closed u →\n  ∀ ρ ∈ Set.Icc (-1 : ℝ) 1,\n    mutualInformation (softJoint ρ u) = info (noise ρ u)"
    },

      "docstring": null,

      "statement_lean": "def RefinedBoolean : Prop := ∀ (n : ℕ) (f : Cube n → Bool)\n  (ρ : ℝ), ρ ∈ Set.Icc (-1 : ℝ) 1 →\n  let m := avg (sign ∘ f)\n  let bound := psi (|ρ| * psiInv (H m))\n  mutualInformation (boolJoint ρ f) = info (noise ρ (sign ∘ f)) ∧\n  mutualInformation (boolJoint ρ f) ≤ bound ∧ bound ≤ psi |ρ| ∧\n  (0 < |m| → |m| < 1 → 0 < |ρ| → bound < psi |ρ|)"

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove sharp contraction of the information carried by a binary channel under independent symmetric noise on a uniform discrete cube. At fixed initial information, a noisy coordinate retains the most information. The Boolean specialization resolves the Courtade–Kumar conjecture and gives an output-entropy refinement. We also establish a stronger mean-dependent entropy-production bound. The proof combines an explicit three-point optimizer for the local joining problem, two entropy capacities, and a common-output thinning inequality, followed by dimension induction and integration along the noise semigroup.

<a id="main:section"></a>
### The contraction theorem and its mechanism

A one-bit summary of independent fair bits can be chosen before those bits pass through independent binary symmetric channels. Which summary retains the most information about the noisy observation? Kumar and Courtade formulated the conjecture that a single input coordinate is optimal at every noise level and in every dimension (Kumar and Courtade 2013; Courtade and Kumar 2014) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[KumarCourtade2013](#ref-KumarCourtade2013), [CourtadeKumar2014](#ref-CourtadeKumar2014)]. We prove a sharp comparison for arbitrary randomized binary summaries, together with a mean-dependent bound on their instantaneous information loss.

Let $\Omega_n=\{-1,1\}^n$ carry uniform measure. All information is measured with natural logarithms. For $r\in[-1,1]$, define $$L=\log2,\qquad
 H(r)=-\frac{1+r}{2}\log\frac{1+r}{2}
      -\frac{1-r}{2}\log\frac{1-r}{2},\qquad \psi(r)=L-H(r),$$ with $0\log0=0$. Thus $H(r)$ is the entropy of a sign of mean $r$. For a soft binary channel $u:\Omega_n\to[-1,1]$, define $$I(u)=H(\mathbb Eu)-\mathbb EH(u)=\mathbb E\psi(u)-\psi(\mathbb Eu).$$ Let $X$ be uniform on $\Omega_n$. If an auxiliary sign $U$ has conditional mean $\mathbb E[U\mid X]=u(X)$, then $I(u)=I(U;X)$. For $-1\le\rho\le1$, let $Y_i=X_iZ_i$, where the signs $Z_i$ are independent of $(X,U)$ and each other, with $\mathbb EZ_i=\rho$. The noise operator is $T_\rho u(y)=\mathbb E[u(X)\mid Y=y]$. Thus $\mathbb E[U\mid Y]=T_\rho u(Y)$ and $I(T_\rho u)=I(U;Y)$.

<a id="stmt-main-soft"></a>
<div id="main:soft" class="theorem">

<a id="main:interpolation"></a>
**Theorem 1** (Sharp soft-channel contraction). *For every finite $n\ge0$, every $u:\Omega_n\to[-1,1]$, and $-1\le\rho\le1$, $$I(T_\rho u)
 \le\psi\!\left(|\rho|\,\psi^{-1}(I(u))\right).
 \label{main:interpolation}$$ The inverse is the nonnegative increasing branch on $[0,L]$. For $n\ge1$, every soft coordinate $u(x)=a x_i$, $|a|\le1$, attains equality.*

</div>

The parameter $\psi^{-1}(I(u))$ is the amplitude of a fair soft coordinate with the same information as $u$. The theorem says that this effective amplitude contracts at least as fast as a coordinate. Sharpness concerns all soft channels at a given initial information; it does not assert that every biased Boolean source attains the bound.

<a id="stmt-main-ck"></a>
<div id="main:ck" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (The Boolean bound with output bias). *For every finite $n\ge0$, $\rho\in[-1,1]$, and $f:\Omega_n\to\{-1,1\}$, put $m=\mathbb Ef$ and $r_m=\psi^{-1}(H(m))$. Then <a id="main:bound"></a>$$I(f(X);Y)\le\psi(|\rho|r_m)\le\psi(|\rho|).
 \label{main:bound}$$ For $n\ge1$, signed coordinate functions attain the bound at $m=0$. If $0<|m|<1$ and $0<|\rho|\le1$, the refined upper curve is strictly below the universal curve $\psi(|\rho|)$.*

</div>

For noise crossover probability $\varepsilon\in[0,1/2]$, one has $\rho=1-2\varepsilon$. Writing $h_2(p)=-p\log_2p-(1-p)\log_2(1-p)$, the universal bound in bits is $$I_2(f(X);Y)\le\frac{\psi(\rho)}{L}=1-h_2(\varepsilon).$$ This is the Courtade–Kumar conjecture. The refinement uses the actual entropy of the Boolean output, while the input distribution remains uniform.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Prior work and related results

Ordentlich, Shayevitz, and Weinstein (Ordentlich, Shayevitz, and Weinstein 2016) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[OrdentlichShayevitzWeinstein2016](#ref-OrdentlichShayevitzWeinstein2016)] obtained asymptotically sharp high-noise bounds for balanced Boolean functions. Samorodnitsky (Samorodnitsky 2016) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Samorodnitsky2016](#ref-Samorodnitsky2016)] proved the conjecture for all Boolean functions in a dimension-independent high-noise range. Yu’s work on $\Phi$-stability and local optimality (Yu 2023, 2026) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Yu2023](#ref-Yu2023), [Yu2026](#ref-Yu2026)] enlarged the proved range for balanced functions; the latter gives $0\le\rho\le0.914$ by a computer-assisted argument. Javanmard and Woodruff (Javanmard and Woodruff 2026) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[JavanmardWoodruff2026](#ref-JavanmardWoodruff2026)] extended Courtade and Kumar’s coordinatewise sum bound (Courtade and Kumar 2014, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[CourtadeKumar2014](#ref-CourtadeKumar2014)] from balanced functions to arbitrary output bias and refined high-noise entropy estimates.

Several nearby formulations clarify the role of the full noisy vector. Pichler, Piantanida, and Matz (Pichler, Piantanida, and Matz 2018) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[PichlerPiantanidaMatz2018](#ref-PichlerPiantanidaMatz2018)] proved coordinate optimality when the observation is also reduced to one Boolean output. At fixed output mean, Li and Médard (Li and Médard 2021) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[LiMedard2021](#ref-LiMedard2021)] identified lexicographic optimizers at sufficiently low noise and related high-noise optimizers to first-level Fourier weight, in dimension-dependent ranges. Barnes and Özgür (Barnes and Özgür 2020) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BarnesOzgur2020](#ref-BarnesOzgur2020)] proved the equivalence of the balanced conjecture and a symmetrized Li–Médard moment inequality. Kindler, O’Donnell, and Witmer (Kindler, O’Donnell, and Witmer 2016) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KindlerODonnellWitmer2016](#ref-KindlerODonnellWitmer2016)] proved fixed-mean Gaussian and spherical analogues. These fixed-mean questions are distinct from attainability of the refined upper curve in Corollary <a href="#main:ck" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:ck">2</a>.

Chen, Gohari, and Nair (Chen, Gohari, and Nair 2025) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[ChenGohariNair2025](#ref-ChenGohariNair2025)] <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GohariNair2022](#ref-GohariNair2022)] developed the differential approach that we use: prove a local entropy-production inequality, lift it by induction on the cube dimension, and integrate along the noise semigroup. Their approach builds on the auxiliary receiver method of Gohari and Nair (Gohari and Nair 2022). The August 2026 revision of Chen–Gohari–Nair disproves one proposed auxiliary inequality (Chen, Gohari, and Nair 2025, Appendix B) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[ChenGohariNair2025](#ref-ChenGohariNair2025)]; the local comparisons below are proved directly. Their symmetric optimizer is a predecessor of our arbitrary-mean joining problem, as explained in Section <a href="#canonical:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="canonical:section">3</a>.

Recent preprints announce proofs of the Courtade–Kumar conjecture by several routes. Chen, Gohari, Javanmard, Lin, Mirrokni, Nair, and Woodruff (Chen et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026CK](#ref-ChenEtAl2026CK)] use a computer-assisted Bellman inequality; Ky and Tran (Ky and Tran 2026) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[KyTran2026CK](#ref-KyTran2026CK)] develop entropy-production and spectral estimates with computer-assisted verification; Mahdavifar and Beirami (Mahdavifar and Beirami 2026) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[MahdavifarBeirami2026CK](#ref-MahdavifarBeirami2026CK)] use entropy flow, Fourier analysis, and bounds for pivotal sets; and Kramer and Saglam (Kramer and Saglam 2026) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[KramerSaglam2026](#ref-KramerSaglam2026)] prove a finite-noise bound that retains the Boolean output bias. The overlap with the first work extends beyond the Boolean conjecture: its Lemma 9.2 applies to arbitrary binary channels, and retaining the initial information when integrating its production bound gives Theorem <a href="#main:soft" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:soft">1</a> and Corollary <a href="#main:ck" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:ck">2</a>. Our proof solves the joining problem with a common entropy budget and uses two capacities and common-output thinning. It also proves the additional mean-dependent production estimate in Theorem <a href="#upgrade:production" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:production">3</a>; we compare the two production profiles in Section <a href="#flow:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flow:comparison">8.1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### From information loss to a local joining inequality

<a id="main:flow-identity"></a>
For an interior soft field $g:\Omega_n\to(-1,1)$, put <a id="main:functionals"></a>$$m=\mathbb Eg,\qquad e=\mathbb EH(g),\qquad I(g)=H(m)-e,
 \qquad D(g)=\mathbb E[V(g)Ng],
 \label{main:functionals}$$ where $V(r)=\operatorname{atanh}r$ and $$N=\frac12\sum_{i=1}^n(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{flip}_i),
 \qquad P_t=T_{e^{-t}}=e^{-tN}.$$ Here $\mathrm{flip}_i$ flips coordinate $i$. Mean conservation and finite-dimensional differentiation give $$\frac{d}{dt}I(P_tg)=-D(P_tg).
 \label{main:flow-identity}$$ The production of a soft coordinate $g(x)=r x_i$ is $rV(r)$. We therefore define its rate by $F(\psi(r))=rV(r)$ for $0\le r<1$ and seek the inequality $D(g)\ge F(I(g))$. Once this is proved, $r_t=\psi^{-1}(I(P_tg))$ satisfies $r_t'\le-r_t$ whenever $I(P_tg)>0$. Integrating gives Theorem <a href="#main:soft" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:soft">1</a>; Section <a href="#flow:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flow:section">8</a> treats zero information and boundary-valued inputs.

<a id="main:energy-split"></a>
The local-to-global program is due to Chen–Gohari–Nair (Chen, Gohari, and Nair 2025, Definition 1 and Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[ChenGohariNair2025](#ref-ChenGohariNair2025)]. To see the required local comparison, split the cube along one coordinate into fields $g_+,g_-$ on $\Omega_{n-1}$. Their productions satisfy the exact identity $$D(g)=\frac{D(g_+)+D(g_-)}2+\mathbb Ec(g_+,g_-),\qquad
 c(a,b)=\frac{a-b}{4}\{V(a)-V(b)\},
 \label{main:energy-split}$$ where the last expectation is over the remaining coordinates. Write $m_\pm=\mathbb Eg_\pm$ and $I_\pm=I(g_\pm)$. For a profile with $\Phi_m(0)=0$, the required induction step is that the joining edges pay its increase: $$\mathbb Ec(g_+,g_-)
 \ge\Phi_m(I(g))-
 \frac{\Phi_{m_+}(I_+)+\Phi_{m_-}(I_-)}2.$$ The information-only choice $\Phi_m(I)=F(I)$ does not satisfy the corresponding local comparison for every coupled pair law (Chen, Gohari, and Nair 2025, Remark 5) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[ChenGohariNair2025](#ref-ChenGohariNair2025)]. The proof must retain the mean as well as the information.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Two profiles and the geometry of the joining cost

The linear estimate $D(g)\ge2I(g)$ follows from the classical cube logarithmic Sobolev inequality and its entropy-dissipation consequence, within the hypercontractive framework of Bonami, Gross, and Beckner (Bonami 1970; Gross 1975; Beckner 1975) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Bonami1970](#ref-Bonami1970), [Gross1975](#ref-Gross1975), [Beckner1975](#ref-Beckner1975)]; see also Bobkov–Tetali (Bobkov and Tetali 2006, Example 3.7) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BobkovTetali2006](#ref-BobkovTetali2006)] for the modified logarithmic Sobolev formulation. Mrs. Gerber’s lemma (Wyner and Ziv 1973) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[WynerZiv1973](#ref-WynerZiv1973)] contains the same scalar one-bit entropy curve, but its direct vector form has dimension-normalized arguments. The nonlinear surplus above $2I$ is the part that needs a new local comparison.

<a id="main:profile"></a>
Since $F'(0)=2$, define $$S(I)=F(I)-2I\quad(0\le I<L),\qquad S(I)=0\quad(I<0).$$ For $|m|<1$ and $0\le I<H(m)$, set $$\mathcal B_m(I)=2I+(1-m^2)S\!\left(
 L-\frac{H(m)-I}{1-m^2}\right).
 \label{main:profile}$$ The factor $1-m^2$ is useful because the average of the two child variance factors at means $m\pm\delta$ is $1-m^2-\delta^2$. Convexity therefore pools their surplus terms in the same form. Section <a href="#reserve:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="reserve:section">5</a> explains the two-capacity allocation behind this profile and proves its local joining inequality. The same two capacities occur in the Boolean finite-noise bound of Kramer and Saglam (Kramer and Saglam 2026) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[KramerSaglam2026](#ref-KramerSaglam2026)]; that section gives the comparison.

To prove the local joining inequality for $\mathcal B$, regard $(g_+(X'),g_-(X'))$ as a coupled pair of random variables, where $X'$ is uniform on the remaining cube. We minimize the joining cost over all coupled pair laws with the same child means and average conditional entropy, extending $c$ by zero at the equal corners and by $+\infty$ at every other boundary pair. The exact optimizer has one interior pair $(a_c,b_c)$ and possibly the two equal-output corners $(1,1)$ and $(-1,-1)$. Section <a href="#canonical:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="canonical:section">3</a> proves this description by assigning a nonnegative price to entropy. Section <a href="#capacity:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="capacity:section">4</a> proves the one-bit inequality that pays the interior pair. Adding either common output reduces the payment required by $\mathcal B$ at least in proportion to the retained mass. Composing these two operations pays the full optimizer. Throughout this minimization, the original face entropies remain in the child profiles; only their average enters the cost problem.

<a id="main:threshold"></a>
At mean zero, $\mathcal B_0=F$, but at other means neither profile can be discarded. We prove local joining for their maximum, <a id="main:hybrid-profile"></a>$$\widehat{\mathcal B}_m(I)
 =\max\{\mathcal B_m(I),F(I)\}.
 \label{main:hybrid-profile}$$ Two comparisons make this possible. First, $$I\ge L/2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathcal B_m(I)\ge F(I).
 \label{main:threshold}$$ Thus only parents below half information can require the $F$ branch. At such a parent, convexity at the original child states reduces the unpaid amount to $F(I)-F(j)$, where $j=(I_++I_-)/2$.

For the second comparison, reflect the output signs if necessary so that the parent mean is nonnegative. An optimizer with one corner then combines a core of nonnegative center with $(1,1)$. Section <a href="#thinning:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thinning:section">6</a> shows that this addition reduces the increment at least in proportion to the retained mass, and so the one-bit inequality pays the mixture. When both corners occur, the core is reflected, $(r,-r)$. Holding the core and its mass fixed preserves the separation of the child means, their average conditional entropy, and the joining cost. We increase the common mean until one corner disappears, as in Figure <a href="#fig:reflected-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:reflected-core">1</a>. Below half information the required increment increases during this motion. The one-corner endpoint therefore pays the original state as well.

Section <a href="#upgrade:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:section">7</a> combines these comparisons and performs dimension induction to prove the following strengthening of the scalar rate inequality.

<a id="stmt-upgrade-production"></a>
<div id="upgrade:production" class="theorem">

**Theorem 3** (Sharp soft production). *For every finite $n\ge0$ and every $g:\{-1,1\}^n\to(-1,1)$, with $m=\mathbb Eg$, <a id="upgrade:production-bound"></a>$$D(g)\ge\widehat{\mathcal B}_m(I(g))
       =\max\{\mathcal B_m(I(g)),F(I(g))\}
       \ge F(I(g)).
 \label{upgrade:production-bound}$$*

</div>

The scalar facts needed by the local comparisons are proved in Section <a href="#scalar:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:section">2</a>. Section <a href="#flow:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flow:section">8</a> integrates the production bound and compares its mean-dependent part with the profile of Chen et al. (Chen et al. 2026) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026CK](#ref-ChenEtAl2026CK)].

<a id="scalar:section"></a>
### The scalar rate and its curvature

A soft coordinate fixes the rate used throughout the proof. We need its convexity, two bounds on its entropy-scaled curvature, and one comparison of logarithmic slopes. These facts follow from a positive hyperbolic integral and two elementary moment comparisons.

<a id="scalar:surplus-definition"></a>
All logarithms are natural. For a sign with mean $r\in[-1,1]$, put <a id="scalar:entropy-definitions"></a>$$\label{scalar:entropy-definitions}
 L=\log 2,\qquad
 \psi(r)=\frac{(1+r)\log(1+r)+(1-r)\log(1-r)}2,\qquad
 H(r)=L-\psi(r),$$ with $0\log0=0$. Thus $H$ is binary entropy and $\psi$ is its gap from the entropy of a fair sign. Write $V(r)=\operatorname{atanh}r$ for $-1<r<1$. Then <a id="scalar:entropy-derivatives"></a>$$\label{scalar:entropy-derivatives}
 \psi'(r)=V(r),\qquad \psi''(r)=\frac1{1-r^2},\qquad H'(r)=-V(r).$$ The restriction of $\psi$ to $[0,1)$ increases bijectively onto $[0,L)$. Define the production rate and its surplus over the linear rate by <a id="scalar:profile-definitions"></a>$$\label{scalar:profile-definitions}
 F(\psi(r))=rV(r)\qquad(0\le r<1),$$ $$\label{scalar:surplus-definition}
 S(I)=
 \begin{cases}
  F(I)-2I,&0\le I<L,\\
  0,&I<0.
 \end{cases}$$

<a id="stmt-scalar-entropy-bounds"></a>
<div id="scalar:entropy-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Elementary entropy bounds). *For $|r|\le1$, <a id="scalar:entropy-quadratic-bounds"></a>$$\label{scalar:entropy-quadratic-bounds}
 \frac{r^2}{2}\le\psi(r)\le Lr^2,\qquad H(r)\ge L(1-r^2).$$ The quotient $\psi(r)/r^2$ is strictly increasing for $0<r<1$. Moreover, <a id="scalar:logarithm-constants"></a>$$\label{scalar:logarithm-constants}
 \frac23<L<\frac{25}{36}<\frac7{10},\qquad
 \frac12<M:=\frac{4L^2}{3}<\frac23.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scalar-entropy-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-scalar-entropy-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Integrating the geometric series for $\psi''$ twice gives <a id="scalar:entropy-series"></a>$$\label{scalar:entropy-series}
 \psi(r)=\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{r^{2n}}{2n(2n-1)}.$$ The first term gives the lower bound. The coefficients are positive and sum to $\psi(1)=L$, so $r^{2n}\le r^2$ gives the upper bound. The same series proves the quotient’s strict increase. Subtracting from $L$ gives the bound for $H$. Finally, $L=2\operatorname{atanh}(1/3)$ implies $$\frac23<L
 =\frac23+2\sum_{n\ge1}\frac{3^{-(2n+1)}}{2n+1}
 <\frac23+\frac23\sum_{n\ge1}3^{-(2n+1)}
 =\frac{25}{36}.$$ Hence $16/27<M<625/972<2/3$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-scalar-profile-convexity"></a>
<div id="scalar:profile-convexity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Convexity of the rate and surplus). *The function $F$ is smooth on $[0,L)$, with right derivatives at zero, and <a id="scalar:production-convexity"></a>$$\label{scalar:production-convexity}
 F(0)=0,\qquad F'(0)=2,\qquad F''(0)=\frac43,\qquad
 F''(I)\ge\frac43,\qquad F'''(I)\ge0.$$ The zero-extended surplus $S$ is nonnegative, nondecreasing, convex, and $C^1$ on $(-\infty,L)$, with $S(0)=S'(0)=0$. It is not $C^2$ at zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scalar-profile-convexity"><summary>Proof of stmt-scalar-profile-convexity</summary>

<a id="scalar:production-second-derivative"></a>
<a id="scalar:production-positive-series"></a>
*Proof.* Put $r=\tanh v$ and $I=\psi(r)$. Direct differentiation gives $$I=v\tanh v-\log\cosh v,\qquad
 \frac{dI}{dv}=v\operatorname{sech}^2v,\qquad
 F'(I)=1+\frac{\sinh(2v)}{2v}.$$ For $v>0$, another differentiation yields $$\begin{aligned}
\label{scalar:production-second-derivative}
 F''(\psi(r))
 &=\frac{(1+r^2)V(r)-r}{(1-r^2)^2V(r)^3}\\
 &=\cosh^2v\,A(v),\qquad
 A(v):=2\int_0^1(1-s^2)\cosh(2vs)\,ds.
\label{scalar:production-positive-series}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, integration by parts evaluates the integral as $[2v\cosh(2v)-\sinh(2v)]/(2v^3)$. The integral gives $A(v)\ge A(0)=4/3$ and $A'(v)>0$ for $v>0$. Thus $F''\ge4/3$ and $F'''>0$ for $I>0$. Both $A(v)$ and $I$ are analytic in $v^2$, and $I=v^2/2+O(v^4)$, so the inverse function theorem gives smooth extension to $I=0$. Continuity gives $F'''(0)\ge0$. The identities $S(0)=S'(0)=0$ and $S''=F''>0$ for $I>0$ prove the assertions about its zero extension. Its second derivative jumps from $0$ to $4/3$ at zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-scalar-curvature-growth"></a>
<div id="scalar:curvature-growth" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Growth of the curvature). *The function $I\mapsto e^{-2I}F''(I)$ is strictly increasing on $[0,L)$. For $I>0$ it satisfies $$\frac{F'''(I)}{F''(I)}>2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scalar-curvature-growth"><summary>Proof of stmt-scalar-curvature-growth</summary>

*Proof.* Using <a href="#scalar:production-positive-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="scalar:production-positive-series">[scalar:production-positive-series]</a>, for $v>0$, $$\frac{d}{dI}\log F''(I)
 =\frac{\sinh(2v)}v
  +\frac{\cosh^2v}{v}\frac{A'(v)}{A(v)}>2.$$ Here $A'(v)\ge0$ and $\sinh(2v)>2v$. Continuity at zero extends the asserted strict increase to $[0,L)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-scalar-scaled-curvature"></a>
<div id="scalar:scaled-curvature" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Scaled entropy curvature). *For $0<h\le L$, put <a id="scalar:scaled-curvature-definition"></a>$$\label{scalar:scaled-curvature-definition}
 P(h)=h^2F''(L-h).$$ Then <a id="scalar:scaled-curvature-range"></a>$$\label{scalar:scaled-curvature-range}
 \frac12\le P(h)\le M=\frac{4L^2}{3},\qquad P(L)=M.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-scalar-scaled-curvature"><summary>Proof of stmt-scalar-scaled-curvature</summary>

*Proof.* Write $h=H(r)$, $t=r^2$, and $x=V(r)/r$, with $x=1$ at $r=0$. Elementary integration gives <a id="scalar:entropy-moment-integrals"></a>$$\label{scalar:entropy-moment-integrals}
 x=\int_0^1\frac{du}{1-tu^2},\qquad
 \frac{H(r)}{1-t}=\int_0^1\frac{du}{(1+u)(1-tu^2)}.$$ For the second identity, use the partial fraction identity $$\frac1{(1+u)(1-tu^2)}
 =\frac1{1-t}\left(\frac1{1+u}+\frac{t(u-1)}{1-tu^2}\right)$$ and $H(r)=L-\frac12\log(1-r^2)-rV(r)$. Let $\mu_t$ have density $[x(1-tu^2)]^{-1}$ on $[0,1]$, and set $$b_t=\mathbb E_{\mu_t}\frac1{1+u},\qquad n_t=\mathbb E_{\mu_t}u^2.$$ Then $b_t=H(r)/[(1-t)x]$ and $n_t=(V(r)-r)/(r^2V(r))$, with continuous values at zero. Substitution into <a href="#scalar:production-second-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="scalar:production-second-derivative">[scalar:production-second-derivative]</a> gives $$P(H(r))=b_t^2(1+n_t).$$ Jensen’s inequality and Cauchy–Schwarz imply $$b_t\ge\frac1{1+\mathbb E_{\mu_t}u}\ge\frac1{1+\sqrt{n_t}},
 \qquad
 P(H(r))\ge\frac{1+n_t}{(1+\sqrt{n_t})^2}\ge\frac12.$$

<a id="scalar:covariance-differentiation"></a>
For the upper bound, a density proportional to $w(u)/(1-tu^2)$, with $w$ independent of $t$, satisfies $$\label{scalar:covariance-differentiation}
 \frac{d}{dt}\mathbb E_t f(u)
 =\operatorname{Cov}_t\left(f(u),\frac{u^2}{1-tu^2}\right).$$ Oppositely monotone functions have nonpositive covariance, since for independent copies $U,U'$, $$2\operatorname{Cov}(f(U),g(U))
 =\mathbb E[(f(U)-f(U'))(g(U)-g(U'))].$$ Applying this with $w=1$ shows $1/2\le b_t\le b_0=L$. The same identity shows that $n_t$ increases, so we must control its growth together with the decrease of $b_t$. Compare $b_t-1/2$ with $1-n_t$ by reweighting $\mu_t$ by $1-u^2$ to obtain $\nu_t$. The identity $$\beta_t:=\frac{b_t-1/2}{1-n_t}
 =\frac12\mathbb E_{\nu_t}(1+u)^{-2},\qquad
 d\nu_t(u)\ \propto\ \frac{1-u^2}{1-tu^2}\,du$$ and <a href="#scalar:covariance-differentiation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="scalar:covariance-differentiation">[scalar:covariance-differentiation]</a> now give $0<\beta_t\le\beta_0=\frac32(L-\frac12)$. Consequently $$1+n_t\le2-\frac{b_t-1/2}{\beta_0},$$ and hence $$P(H(r))\le f(b_t),\qquad
 f(b)=b^2\left[2-\frac{b-1/2}{\beta_0}\right].$$ For $1/2\le b\le L$, $$f'(b)=\frac b{\beta_0}(4\beta_0+1-3b)
 \ge\frac b{\beta_0}(3L-2)>0.$$ Thus $P(H(r))\le f(L)=4L^2/3$. At $r=0$, the uniform law has $b_0=L$ and $n_0=1/3$, which also gives $P(L)=4L^2/3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="canonical:section"></a>
### A joining cost with one entropy budget

The energy decomposition <a href="#main:energy-split" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:energy-split">[main:energy-split]</a> leaves the cost of joining the two faces of a coordinate split. We minimize this cost at fixed child means and average conditional entropy. Allowing arbitrary coupled pair laws is a valid relaxation because only a lower bound on the actual joining cost is needed; no independence of the faces is assumed. The symmetric fixed-entropy problem of Chen–Gohari–Nair (Chen, Gohari, and Nair 2025, Theorem 4 and Section IV-C) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[ChenGohariNair2025](#ref-ChenGohariNair2025)] has an optimizer supported on a reflected interior pair and the two equal-output corners. Chen et al. (Chen et al. 2026, sec. 3.1, Theorem 3.10) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026CK](#ref-ChenEtAl2026CK)] characterize related three-point optimizers when both means and both entropy moments are prescribed, under explicit contact and mass conditions. Here we prescribe the two means but constrain only the average entropy. Pricing this common entropy budget gives the optimizer throughout the domain, including the transition from two corners to one.

For interior $x,y$, use the joining cost <a id="canonical:cost"></a>$$c(x,y)=\frac{x-y}{4}\,[V(x)-V(y)].
 \label{canonical:cost}$$ It is symmetric and nonnegative. Extend it by zero at the equal corners $(1,1),(-1,-1)$ and by $+\infty$ at every other boundary pair. This is a lower semicontinuous, jointly convex function. Indeed, $$c(x,y)=\frac18\{J(1+x,1+y)+J(1-x,1-y)\},
 \qquad J(s,t)=(s-t)\log(s/t),$$ and $J$ is the sum of two relative-entropy perspectives.

<a id="canonical:budget"></a>
Fix $-1<b<a<1$, and write $$m=\frac{a+b}{2},\qquad \delta=\frac{a-b}{2},\qquad
 e_{\max}=\frac{H(a)+H(b)}2.$$ Jensen’s inequality makes $e_{\max}$ the largest possible average entropy; the deterministic pair attains it. We call this boundary the capacity state of the joining problem. For $0<e\le e_{\max}$, define $$\mathcal K(m,\delta,e)=
 \inf\left\{\mathbb Ec(A,B):
 \mathbb EA=a,\ \mathbb EB=b,\
 \frac{\mathbb EH(A)+\mathbb EH(B)}2\le e\right\}.
 \label{canonical:budget}$$ The infimum is over joint laws on $[-1,1]^2$. Only the average entropy is constrained.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Constructing and certifying the priced core

<a id="canonical:law"></a>
We first construct candidate laws of the form $$\lambda\delta_{(x,y)}
 +\pi_+\delta_{(1,1)}+\pi_-\delta_{(-1,-1)},\qquad -1<y<x<1.
 \label{canonical:law}$$ The prescribed mean difference forces $\lambda(x-y)=2\delta$; the mean sum and total mass then determine the two corner masses. We will minimize a priced objective within this family, and then prove optimality against all coupled laws by an affine lower bound.

<a id="canonical:coordinates"></a>
<a id="canonical:masses"></a>
<a id="canonical:floors"></a>
For an ordered interior pair $x>y$, set $$z=\log\frac{1+x}{1+y},\quad
 w=\log\frac{1-y}{1-x},\quad
 u=(e^z-1)^{-1},\quad v=(e^w-1)^{-1},\quad n=1+u+v.$$ Then $$x=\frac{1+u-v}{n},\qquad
 y=\frac{u-v-1}{n},\qquad x-y=\frac2n,\qquad
 \frac{c(x,y)}{x-y}=\frac{z+w}{8}.
 \label{canonical:coordinates}$$ In these coordinates, the masses required by the two means are $$\lambda=\delta n,\qquad
 \pi_+=\frac{1+b-2\delta u}{2},\qquad
 \pi_-=\frac{1-a-2\delta v}{2}.
 \label{canonical:masses}$$ They sum to one, and the corner masses are nonnegative exactly when $$z\ge z_0:=\log\frac{1+a}{1+b},\qquad
 w\ge w_0:=\log\frac{1-b}{1-a}.
 \label{canonical:floors}$$ For a price $p\ge0$, define $$\mathcal H(z,w)=\frac{H(x)+H(y)}{x-y},\qquad
 F_p(z,w)=\frac{z+w}{8}+p\mathcal H(z,w).$$ The corners contribute neither cost nor entropy. Hence the priced objective of the candidate law <a href="#canonical:law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:law">[canonical:law]</a> is <a id="canonical:candidate-value"></a>$$\mathbb E\{c(A,B)+p[H(A)+H(B)]\}=2\delta F_p(z,w).
 \label{canonical:candidate-value}$$ This explains the normalization by $x-y$: the prescribed mean difference absorbs the core mass. The remaining task is to minimize $F_p$ subject to <a href="#canonical:floors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:floors">[canonical:floors]</a> and certify that its value also bounds every other pair law.

<a id="canonical:entropy-series"></a>
The normalized entropy has the positive series $$\begin{split}
 \mathcal H(z,w)
 &=\frac{q(z)+q(w)}2+
 \sum_{i,j\ge1}\frac{e^{-iz-jw}}{\max(i,j)},\\
 q(t)&=\frac{t}{e^t-1}-\log(1-e^{-t}).
\end{split}
 \label{canonical:entropy-series}$$ Here is a direct verification. The normalized entropy of $x$ is $[n\log n-(u+1)\log(u+1)-v\log v]/2$, and that of $y$ is its transpose. Put $\xi=e^{-z}$ and $\zeta=e^{-w}$. After subtracting $[q(z)+q(w)]/2$, the sum becomes $$n\log(1-\xi\zeta)-v\log(1-\xi)-u\log(1-\zeta).$$ The coefficient of $\xi^i\zeta^j$ is $1/i+1/j-1/\min(i,j)=1/\max(i,j)$, which proves <a href="#canonical:entropy-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:entropy-series">[canonical:entropy-series]</a>. The series and its derivatives converge uniformly on compact subsets of the positive quadrant.

The function $\mathcal H$ is symmetric and strictly convex. In fact, with $v_t=(e^t-1)^{-1}$, $$q''(t)=v_t(v_t+1)\{t(2v_t+1)-1\}>0,$$ because $t(2v_t+1)=t\coth(t/2)>2$. Each term in the double series is convex; its exponential direction vectors span the plane.

<a id="stmt-canonical-priced"></a>
<div id="canonical:priced" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (The priced core). *For every $p\ge0$, the minimum of $$\mathbb E\{c(A,B)+p[H(A)+H(B)]\},
 \qquad \mathbb EA=a,\quad \mathbb EB=b,$$ has a unique optimizer. It is one ordered interior pair $(x,y)$ and possible equal corners, as in <a href="#canonical:law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:law">[canonical:law]</a>. Its core is given by the unique minimizer of $F_p$ subject to <a href="#canonical:floors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:floors">[canonical:floors]</a>, using <a href="#canonical:coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:coordinates">[canonical:coordinates]</a>; its masses are <a href="#canonical:masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:masses">[canonical:masses]</a>. At $p=0$, the optimizer is the deterministic pair $(a,b)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-canonical-priced"><summary>Proof of stmt-canonical-priced</summary>

<a id="canonical:support"></a>
*Proof.* For $p>0$, strict convexity and the linear term give a unique minimizer on the floored quadrant. At $p=0$, the unique minimum is $(z_0,w_0)$. We now turn this constrained minimum into an affine lower bound in the pair values, valid for every coupled law. At the selected point put $$\gamma_+=\frac{(F_p)_z}{2u(u+1)},\qquad
 \gamma_-=\frac{(F_p)_w}{2v(v+1)}.$$ Both are nonnegative; a derivative vanishes unless its floor is active. The function $$\mathscr Q(z,w)=F_p(z,w)+\gamma_+(1+2u)+\gamma_-(1+2v)$$ has zero gradient at the selected point and is strictly convex on the whole positive quadrant. At $p=0$, both added coefficients are positive, and $u''=u(u+1)(2u+1)>0$, with the same identity for $v$, so strict convexity still holds. Let $\kappa>0$ be its minimum; positivity follows from the positive linear term in $F_p$ and the nonnegative remaining terms. Multiplying $\mathscr Q\ge\kappa$ by $x-y$ gives the global support $$\begin{split}
 c(x,y)+p[H(x)+H(y)]-\kappa(x-y)
 +\gamma_+(2+x+y)+\gamma_-(2-x-y)\ge0.
\end{split}
 \label{canonical:support}$$ For $x>y$ this is the convex minimum just proved. For $x<y$, every term is nonnegative and $-\kappa(x-y)>0$. On the interior diagonal, the entropy term is positive if $p>0$; if $p=0$, both corner coefficients are positive. At the equal corners, equality is allowed exactly when the corresponding $\gamma$ vanishes. All other boundary pairs have infinite cost.

The candidate masses in <a href="#canonical:masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:masses">[canonical:masses]</a> have means $a,b$, and complementarity gives $\gamma_\pm\pi_\pm=0$. Thus <a href="#canonical:law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:law">[canonical:law]</a> is supported at equality points of <a href="#canonical:support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:support">[canonical:support]</a>. Averaging the support proves optimality. Its only possible interior contact is the unique convex minimizer; the mean difference fixes its mass, and the mean sum fixes the two corners. This proves uniqueness. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-canonical-coverage"></a>
<div id="canonical:coverage" class="proposition">

**Proposition 9** (Exact entropy coverage). *For every $0<e\le e_{\max}$, a law of the form <a href="#canonical:law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:law">[canonical:law]</a> attains <a href="#canonical:budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:budget">[canonical:budget]</a> and has average entropy exactly $e$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-canonical-coverage"><summary>Proof of stmt-canonical-coverage</summary>

*Proof.* Let $e(p)$ be the average entropy of the unique priced optimizer. It is continuous for $p\ge0$: on each bounded price interval the minimizers lie in a common compact subset of the floored quadrant, since $F_p\ge(z+w)/8$; uniqueness then gives continuity. Also $e(0)=e_{\max}$.

There are finite-cost laws with the prescribed means and arbitrarily small entropy. Choose a reflected core $(r,-r)$ with $r$ sufficiently close to one, mass $\delta/r$, and the two equal-corner masses needed to match $m$. They are nonnegative because $\delta<1-|m|$. Its entropy $(\delta/r)H(r)$ tends to zero. If such a comparison law has cost $C_\varepsilon$ and entropy at most $\varepsilon$, priced optimality and nonnegativity of cost imply $$2p\,e(p)\le C_\varepsilon+2p\varepsilon.$$ Hence $e(p)\to0$ as $p\to\infty$. Continuity supplies a price with $e(p)=e$.

For any competing law whose average entropy $\widetilde e$ is at most $e$, the global priced support gives $$\mathbb Ec(A,B)+2p\widetilde e
 \ge \mathbb Ec(A_p,B_p)+2pe.$$ Since $p\ge0$, its cost is at least that of the priced optimizer. This proves the proposition without a signed entropy multiplier or an abstract optimizer replacement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The two canonical geometries

By reflection assume $m\ge0$, so $z_0\le w_0$. Symmetry and uniqueness imply $z\le w$ at the priced minimizer: otherwise swapping the coordinates is feasible and has the same value. The case $p=0$ is already deterministic, so assume $p>0$ in the following strict-convexity argument. If $w>w_0$ and $z<w$, then $(F_p)_w=0$ while strict convexity and symmetry give $(F_p)_z<(F_p)_w$, contradicting constrained optimality. Thus either $z=w$ or $w=w_0$.

<a id="canonical:reflected"></a>
In the first case, the core is $(r,-r)$, with $$k=\frac{\delta}{r},\qquad
 e=kH(r),\qquad
 \mathcal K=\delta V(r),\qquad 0\le m\le1-k.
 \label{canonical:reflected}$$ At fixed $\delta,e$, the radius $r$ and the cost do not depend on $m$. Uniqueness of $r$ follows because $H(r)/r$ strictly decreases.

<a id="canonical:onecorner"></a>
<a id="canonical:transition"></a>
In the second case, $\pi_-=0$. With core mass $k$, $$\begin{split}
 1-m\le k\le1,\qquad
 x=1-\frac{1-a}{k},\quad y=1-\frac{1-b}{k},\\
 e=\frac{k}{2}[H(x)+H(y)],\qquad
 \mathcal K=k\,c(x,y).
\end{split}
 \label{canonical:onecorner}$$ Indeed $z\le w$ means the core center is nonnegative, and $$\frac{x+y}{2}=1-\frac{1-m}{k}\ge0.$$ The two geometries meet at $k=1-m$, where the reflected core has only a positive common corner. Thus their entropy threshold is $$e_0(m,\delta)=(1-m)H\!\left(\frac{\delta}{1-m}\right).
 \label{canonical:transition}$$ To check the direction of the threshold, differentiate the one-corner entropy in <a href="#canonical:onecorner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:onecorner">[canonical:onecorner]</a> at fixed $a,b$: $$\frac{d}{dk}\frac{k}{2}[H(x)+H(y)]
 =\frac12\left[\log\frac2{1+x}+\log\frac2{1+y}\right]>0.$$ The reflected entropy $kH(\delta/k)$ also strictly increases in $k$, since its derivative is $H(r)+rV(r)>0$. Consequently the reflected geometry has $e\le e_0$, and the one-corner geometry has $e\ge e_0$. This is a support threshold for the exact cost optimizer, separate from the analytic threshold <a href="#main:threshold" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:threshold">[main:threshold]</a> for the production profile. At capacity $k=1$ and the law is deterministic. If $\delta=0$, taking $A=B$ with mean $m$ and arbitrarily small entropy gives $\mathcal K=0$; that case requires no canonical classification.

<figure id="fig:reflected-core">

<figcaption>Mixture weights for a fixed reflected core $(r,−r)$. The horizontal segment keeps its mass $k$ fixed, so the separation $δ = kr$, entropy $e = kH(r)$, and joining cost $δV(r)$ stay fixed. Moving right increases the mean $m = π_{+} − π_{−}$ until the negative common output disappears. Theorem <a href="#upgrade:joining" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:joining">20</a> compares the low-information demand with this endpoint. The triangle is schematic; its points represent probability weights, not channel values.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-reflected-core"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:reflected-core`)

\coordinate (C) at (0,2.8);
\fill[blue!4] (N)--(P)--(C)--cycle;
\draw[gray] (N)--(P)--(C)--cycle;
\node[below left,align=center] at (N) {$\pi_-=1$\\$(-1,-1)$};
\node[below right,align=center] at (P) {$\pi_+=1$\\$(1,1)$};

The bottom-right vertex is labeled "$\pi_+ = 1$" and below it "$(1, 1)$". The left edge of the triangle is labeled "$m = -(1 - k)$". The right edge is labeled "$m = 1 - k$" and "$\pi_- = 0$".
