Figure <a href="#fig:reflected-core" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:reflected-core">1</a> displays the motion within the reflected geometry. The path preserves the optimizer’s cost and its average entropy; the original law’s individual entropies are retained separately when applying the local production inequalities.

<a id="capacity:section"></a>
### The deterministic capacity comparison

The scalar profile is calibrated at balanced one-bit channels. At a biased one-bit channel, the two output labels have unequal posterior correlations. Their separation provides the extra reserve needed by the variance-weighted profile. The inequality below also follows by differentiating at correlation one the pointwise one-bit comparison in the proof of Kramer–Saglam (Kramer and Saglam 2026, Lemma 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[KramerSaglam2026](#ref-KramerSaglam2026)]. We give a direct proof using size-biased posterior information.

<a id="stmt-capacity-onebit"></a>
<div id="capacity:onebit" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (A variance-refined one-bit inequality). *Let $g(X)=m+\delta X$, where $X$ is a uniform sign and $|m|+|\delta|<1$. Write $\alpha=1-m^2$ and $$J=\frac{\psi(m+\delta)+\psi(m-\delta)}2-\psi(m),
 \qquad D=\frac\delta2\{V(m+\delta)-V(m-\delta)\}.$$ Then $J<\alpha L$ and <a id="capacity:refined"></a>$$D\ge\alpha F(J/\alpha).
 \label{capacity:refined}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-capacity-onebit"><summary>Proof of stmt-capacity-onebit</summary>

*Proof.* Reflection and relabeling allow $m\ge0$, $\delta\ge0$. The case $\delta=0$ is immediate, so assume $\delta>0$. Set $$w_\pm=\frac{1\pm m}{2},\qquad
 r_\pm=\frac\delta{1\pm m},\qquad i_\pm=\psi(r_\pm).$$ The two posterior representations of the one-bit channel give <a id="capacity:posterior"></a>$$J=w_+i_++w_-i_-,\qquad
 D=w_+F(i_+)+w_-F(i_-).
 \label{capacity:posterior}$$ For the first identity, introduce a sign $U$ with conditional mean $g(X)$. Its probabilities are $w_\pm$, and the conditional means of $X$ given $U=\pm1$ are $r_+$ and $-r_-$. Evaluating $I(U;X)$ in either order gives the identity. For the second, use $w_\pm r_\pm=\delta/2$ and $$V(m+\delta)-V(m-\delta)=V(r_+)+V(r_-).$$

<a id="capacity:size-biased"></a>
To extract the variance refinement from these identities, use the secant slope $\varphi(s)=F(s)/s$, with $\varphi(0)=2$. It is increasing by convexity of $F$ and $F(0)=0$. It is also convex: the scalar fact $F'''\ge0$ gives, for $s>0$, $$\varphi''(s)
 =\frac{s^2F''(s)-2sF'(s)+2F(s)}{s^3}
 =\frac1{s^3}\int_0^s t^2F'''(t)\,dt\ge0.$$ Since $\delta>0$, both $i_\pm$ and $J$ are positive. The size-biased weights $w_\pm i_\pm/J$ therefore sum to one. Their mean information is $$s_*:=\frac{w_+i_+^2+w_-i_-^2}{J},\qquad
 0<s_*\le\max\{i_+,i_-\}<L.$$ Jensen’s inequality now gives $$\frac DJ
 =\frac{w_+i_+}{J}\varphi(i_+)
  +\frac{w_-i_-}{J}\varphi(i_-)
 \ge\varphi(s_*).
 \label{capacity:size-biased}$$ It remains to show $s_*\ge J/\alpha$. This will also establish $J/\alpha<L$ before we evaluate the rate there.

The positive series for $\psi$ shows that $\psi(r)/r^2$ increases. Consequently $$q:=\frac{i_+}{i_-}
 \le q_0:=\left(\frac{1-m}{1+m}\right)^2.$$ For $0\le q\le1$, the ratio $$R(q)=\frac{w_+q^2+w_-}{(w_+q+w_-)^2}$$ decreases, since $$R'(q)=\frac{2w_+w_-(q-1)}{(w_+q+w_-)^3}\le0.$$ Direct substitution at $q_0$ therefore gives <a id="capacity:secondmoment"></a>$$\frac{w_+i_+^2+w_-i_-^2}{J^2}
 \ge\frac{1+3m^2}{1-m^2}\ge\frac1\alpha.
 \label{capacity:secondmoment}$$

Thus $J/\alpha\le s_*<L$. Combining <a href="#capacity:size-biased" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="capacity:size-biased">[capacity:size-biased]</a> with monotonicity of $\varphi$ yields $$\frac DJ
 \ge\varphi(s_*)
 \ge\varphi(J/\alpha).$$ This proves both assertions. ◻

</details>

The ordinary one-bit bound follows at once: $$c(m+\delta,m-\delta)\ge\alpha F(J/\alpha)\ge F(J).$$ The last inequality is convexity with $F(0)=0$ and $0<\alpha\le1$. We retain the variance-refined form because it pays the deterministic base of the reserve profile, while its ordinary consequence pays the information increment at a core.

<a id="reserve:section"></a>
### A reserve that survives adding either common output

The linear rate $2I$ leaves a remainder that has a simple behavior under mixing. Write $S=F-2I$, as in <a href="#scalar:surplus-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="scalar:surplus-definition">[scalar:surplus-definition]</a>, and put <a id="reserve:perspective"></a>$$R(v,e)=vS\!\left(L-\frac ev\right),\qquad v,e>0,
 \label{reserve:perspective}$$ using the zero extension of $S$ at negative arguments. The profile <a href="#main:profile" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:profile">[main:profile]</a> is $$\mathcal B_m(I)=2I+R(1-m^2,H(m)-I).$$ Its construction can be read as an allocation of information between two capacities. Set $\alpha=1-m^2$ and $c_m=H(m)-\alpha L\ge0$. Then <a id="reserve:allocation"></a>$$\mathcal B_m(I)=
 \inf_{\substack{u+v=I\\0\le u\le c_m\\0\le v<\alpha L}}
 \{2u+\alpha F(v/\alpha)\},\qquad 0\le I<H(m).
 \label{reserve:allocation}$$ Indeed, $F'\ge2$ makes the optimum $u=\min\{I,c_m\}$. The capacity $c_m$ is paid at the linear rate, while the remaining capacity $\alpha L$ uses the exact coordinate rate. This is an identity for the profile, not a decomposition of an actual channel. The finite-noise bound of Kramer and Saglam (Kramer and Saglam 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[KramerSaglam2026](#ref-KramerSaglam2026)] reads, in the present normalization, $$I(T_\rho f)\le\rho^2c_m+\alpha\psi(\rho)
 \qquad(0\le\rho\le1)$$ for a Boolean field $f$ on the uniform cube with $\mathbb Ef=m$. It uses the same coefficients $\alpha$ and $c_m$; here they define the local production profile $\mathcal B_m$.

<a id="reserve:pooling"></a>
<a id="reserve:payment"></a>
To determine the payment required by a join, consider a physical state $$|m|+\delta<1,\quad\delta\ge0,\quad
 0<e\le\bar h:=\frac{H(m+\delta)+H(m-\delta)}2,$$ and write $$\alpha=1-m^2,\quad\beta=\alpha-\delta^2,
 \qquad J=H(m)-\bar h,\quad j=\bar h-e.$$ Set $a=m+\delta$ and $b=m-\delta$. Let $e_a,e_b>0$ satisfy $e_a\le H(a)$, $e_b\le H(b)$, and $(e_a+e_b)/2=e$, and put $I_a=H(a)-e_a$, $I_b=H(b)-e_b$. Their average information is $j$, and their average variance capacity is $[(1-a^2)+(1-b^2)]/2=\beta$. Since $R$ is a convex perspective, the child profiles satisfy $$\frac{\mathcal B_a(I_a)+\mathcal B_b(I_b)}2
 \ge2j+R(\beta,e).
 \label{reserve:pooling}$$ The parent information is $I=H(m)-e=J+j$. Subtracting the pooled bound from $\mathcal B_m(I)=2(J+j)+R(\alpha,e)$ gives the required payment $$W(m,\delta,e)=2J+R(\alpha,e)-R(\beta,e).
 \label{reserve:payment}$$ Thus $$\mathcal B_m(I)-\frac{\mathcal B_a(I_a)+\mathcal B_b(I_b)}2
 \le W(m,\delta,e).$$ It is enough to prove $W\le\mathcal K$. The deterministic capacity comparison pays the interior core; the next two lemmas show that this payment survives adding either common output.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### A logarithmic bound for the corner geometry

<a id="stmt-reserve-geometry"></a>
<div id="reserve:geometry" class="lemma">

<a id="reserve:log-geometry"></a>
**Lemma 11**. *For $-1<m<1$ and $0\le\delta<1-|m|$, put $$A=1-m,\qquad
 \kappa=\frac12\log\frac1{1-\delta^2/(1+m)^2}.$$ Then $$A^2\log\frac\alpha\beta\le3\kappa.
 \label{reserve:log-geometry}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-reserve-geometry"><summary>Proof of stmt-reserve-geometry</summary>

*Proof.* The case $\delta=0$ is immediate. Otherwise set $$h=\frac{1-m}{1+m}>0,\qquad
 t=\frac{\delta^2}{(1+m)^2}<\min\{1,h^2\},\qquad
 f(t)=-\log(1-t).$$ The ratio $f(t/h)/f(t)$ decreases in $t$ when $h\ge1$ and increases when $h\le1$. Indeed, the ratio of the corresponding positive integrands is $(1-t)/(h-t)$, whose derivative has sign $1-h$; the same monotonicity passes to their integral ratio.

If $h\ge1$, the ratio is at most its limit $1/h$ at zero. Thus $A^2f(t/h)/f(t)\le A^2/h=1-m^2\le1$. If $0<h<1$, it suffices to bound the ratio at $t=h^2$. The function $$G(h)=(2-h)f(h)-(2+h)\log(1+h)$$ has $G(0)=0$ and $$G'(h)=2\left[\frac h{1-h^2}-V(h)\right]>0.$$ The last sign follows by bounding the integral defining $V(h)$ by its endpoint integrand. Consequently $$\frac{f(h^2)}{f(h)}
 =1-\frac{\log(1+h)}{f(h)}\ge\frac{2h}{2+h},$$ and $$A^2\frac{f(h)}{f(h^2)}
 \le\frac{2h(2+h)}{(1+h)^2}
 =2-\frac2{(1+h)^2}\le\frac32.$$ Since $f(t)=2\kappa$ and $f(t/h)=\log(\alpha/\beta)$, both cases give <a href="#reserve:log-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:log-geometry">[reserve:log-geometry]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### The common-output contraction

<a id="stmt-reserve-corner"></a>
<div id="reserve:corner" class="lemma">

<a id="reserve:plus-corner"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Adding either common output). *For every physical state $(m,\delta,e)$ and $0<\tau\le1$, $$W(1-\tau+\tau m,\tau\delta,\tau e)
 \le\tau W(m,\delta,e).
 \label{reserve:plus-corner}$$ The same inequality holds with parent mean $-1+\tau+\tau m$, corresponding to the negative common output. There is no sign restriction on the original mean.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-reserve-corner"><summary>Proof of stmt-reserve-corner</summary>

*Proof.* The positive-corner ray scales $A=1-m$, $\delta$, and $e$ by the same factor. Hold $\delta/A$ and $e/A$ fixed, and put $\eta=1+\delta^2/A^2$. Entropy differentiation gives <a id="reserve:entropy-euler"></a>$$A\frac{dJ}{dA}-J=\kappa.
 \label{reserve:entropy-euler}$$ To check this identity, use $H(m)-(1-m)V(m)=\log[2/(1+m)]$; the average of the two child logarithms is the parent logarithm plus $\kappa$.

<a id="reserve:intercept-bound"></a>
For $h>0$, define the entropy intercept $$\gamma(h)=S(L-h)+hS'(L-h)
 =\begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\int_h^L\frac{P(u)}u\,du,&0<h<L,\\
 0,&h\ge L,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad P(u)=u^2F''(L-u).$$ By the scalar curvature bound, $P\le M=4L^2/3$ and $2>3M$. Thus $\gamma\ge0$, and $$0\le\gamma(e/\alpha)-\gamma(e/\beta)
 \le M\log\frac\alpha\beta.
 \label{reserve:intercept-bound}$$ If an argument crosses the clipping point, the integral is simply truncated at $L$.

Since $\alpha=A(2-A)$ and $\beta=A(2-\eta A)$, differentiation of the perspectives in <a href="#reserve:payment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:payment">[reserve:payment]</a> gives <a id="reserve:euler"></a>$$A\frac{dW}{dA}-W
 =2\kappa+A^2\{\eta\gamma(e/\beta)-\gamma(e/\alpha)\}.
 \label{reserve:euler}$$ For example, with $e=Az$, one has $R(\alpha,e)/A=(2-A)S(L-z/(2-A))$, whose derivative is $-\gamma(e/\alpha)$. The second perspective gives the factor $\eta$. The clipped remainder is $C^1$, because $S(0)=S'(0)=0$, so these identities hold across the seam; no second derivative of the zero extension is needed.

Now $\eta\ge1$, <a href="#reserve:intercept-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:intercept-bound">[reserve:intercept-bound]</a>, and Lemma <a href="#reserve:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="reserve:geometry">11</a> give the whole comparison: <a id="reserve:corner-margin"></a>$$A\frac{dW}{dA}-W
 \ge2\kappa-MA^2\log\frac\alpha\beta
 \ge(2-3M)\kappa\ge0.
 \label{reserve:corner-margin}$$ Therefore $W/A$ is nondecreasing. Comparing $A$ and $\tau A$ proves <a href="#reserve:plus-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:plus-corner">[reserve:plus-corner]</a>. The intermediate states remain physical by entropy concavity, and all child means stay interior for $\tau>0$. Finally $W$ is even in $m$, so reflection proves the negative-corner statement. For $\delta=0$, every displayed payment and margin is zero. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### From one core to the local inequality

<a id="stmt-reserve-joining"></a>
<div id="reserve:joining" class="theorem">

**Theorem 13** (Local joining for the linear reserve). *Let $(A,B)$ have any joint law on $[-1,1]^2$ with $e_a=\mathbb EH(A)>0$ and $e_b=\mathbb EH(B)>0$. Put $$a=\mathbb EA,\quad b=\mathbb EB,\quad m=\frac{a+b}{2},
 \quad e=\frac{e_a+e_b}{2},$$ and $I=H(m)-e$, $I_a=H(a)-e_a$, $I_b=H(b)-e_b$. Then <a id="reserve:local-bound"></a>$$\mathbb Ec(A,B)\ge
 \mathcal B_m(I)-\frac{\mathcal B_a(I_a)+\mathcal B_b(I_b)}2.
 \label{reserve:local-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-reserve-joining"><summary>Proof of stmt-reserve-joining</summary>

*Proof.* An infinite cost is immediate. Otherwise positive child entropies give interior means. Swap the children if necessary so that $\delta=(a-b)/2\ge0$; entropy concavity makes $(m,\delta,e)$ physical.

<a id="reserve:capacity"></a>
First pay the deterministic capacity state. For an interior pair $x,y$, let $m_0=(x+y)/2$, $\delta_0=|x-y|/2$, $e_0=[H(x)+H(y)]/2$, and $J_0=H(m_0)-e_0$. Write $\alpha_0=1-m_0^2$, $\beta_0=\alpha_0-\delta_0^2$. The entropy lower bound gives $e_0\ge\beta_0L$ and $$[L-e_0/\alpha_0]_+\le J_0/\alpha_0<L.$$ The last strict inequality and the variance-refined one-bit bound are Lemma <a href="#capacity:onebit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="capacity:onebit">10</a>. Hence $$\begin{aligned}
 W(m_0,\delta_0,e_0)
 &=2J_0+\alpha_0S(L-e_0/\alpha_0)\\
 &\le2J_0+\alpha_0S(J_0/\alpha_0)
 =\alpha_0F(J_0/\alpha_0)\le c(x,y).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{reserve:capacity}$$

<a id="reserve:canonical-payment"></a>
For $\delta>0$, Proposition <a href="#canonical:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="canonical:coverage">9</a> supplies an exact optimizer with interior core $(x,y)$ of mass $k>0$ and equal-corner masses $\pi_+,\pi_-$, where $k+\pi_++\pi_-=1$. Its cost is $k c(x,y)$. Starting from the core, add the positive corner with retention factor $k/(k+\pi_+)$, then the negative corner with retention factor $k+\pi_+$. These two operations produce exactly the canonical law. Lemma <a href="#reserve:corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="reserve:corner">12</a> and <a href="#reserve:capacity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:capacity">[reserve:capacity]</a> give $$W(m,\delta,e)
 \le(k+\pi_+)\frac{k}{k+\pi_+}W(m_0,\delta_0,e_0)
 \le k c(x,y)=\mathcal K(m,\delta,e).
 \label{reserve:canonical-payment}$$ Both retention factors are positive; a missing corner gives a factor one. No orientation or separate case for the core is required. For $\delta=0$, $W=\mathcal K=0$ directly.

Finally the actual pair law has cost at least $\mathcal K(m,\delta,e)$. Combining <a href="#reserve:canonical-payment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:canonical-payment">[reserve:canonical-payment]</a> with the original-child pooling bound <a href="#reserve:pooling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:pooling">[reserve:pooling]</a> gives $$\mathbb Ec(A,B)+\frac{\mathcal B_a(I_a)+\mathcal B_b(I_b)}2
 \ge2(J+j)+R(\alpha,e)=\mathcal B_m(I).$$ All child profiles use their original individual entropies; only the joining cost was minimized with their common average budget. ◻

</details>

The pooling step has a useful exact interpretation. If $e<\beta L$, its minimum over the entropy split is attained at $e_a/(1-a^2)=e_b/(1-b^2)=e/\beta$; these entropies are physical because $H(a)\ge(1-a^2)L$, and similarly for $b$. If $e\ge\beta L$, allocate both child entropies in the intervals $[(1-a^2)L,H(a)]$ and $[(1-b^2)L,H(b)]$ with the prescribed average. Both remainders vanish. Thus the perspective in <a href="#reserve:pooling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="reserve:pooling">[reserve:pooling]</a> is the exact common-budget value of the child reserve, rather than an additional approximation.

<a id="thinning:section"></a>
### Paying the information increment by thinning

The canonical optimizer with one positive common corner has a useful feature: its cost is linear in the mass of its interior core. We will show that the information-rate increment is at most the core’s increment multiplied by that mass. At full mass the increment is paid by the one-bit posterior comparison. This will settle the whole high-entropy branch of the canonical cost problem.

The scalar inputs already proved here are <a id="thinning:scalar-inputs"></a>$$\label{thinning:scalar-inputs}
 F(0)=0,\qquad F'(I)\ge2,\qquad
 0<F''(I)\le\frac{M}{(L-I)^2},\qquad
 M=\frac{4L^2}{3},\qquad 2>3M.$$ Indeed, the slope follows from Lemma <a href="#scalar:profile-convexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:profile-convexity">5</a>, the curvature bound from Lemma <a href="#scalar:scaled-curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:scaled-curvature">7</a>, and the strict margin from Lemma <a href="#scalar:entropy-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:entropy-bounds">4</a>. The proof isolates the roles of these two bounds: the slope supplies a positive term, while the curvature controls the possible loss over an entropy interval.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The common-output path

<a id="thinning:path"></a>
Fix an interior ordered pair $a_c>b_c$ with nonnegative center, and write $$x_c=\frac{a_c+b_c}{2}\ge0,\qquad
 d_c=\frac{a_c-b_c}{2}>0,\qquad
 h_c=\frac{H(a_c)+H(b_c)}2,\qquad
 J_c=H(x_c)-h_c.$$ For $0<\lambda\le1$, put mass $\lambda$ on this core and mass $1-\lambda$ on $(1,1)$. The second atom is a common output: its coordinates agree, and it contributes neither entropy nor cost. The mixture has means $x+d$ and $x-d$, where $$\label{thinning:path}
 x=1-\lambda(1-x_c),\qquad d=\lambda d_c,
 \qquad e=\lambda h_c.$$ For this path define <a id="thinning:information"></a>$$\label{thinning:information}
 \bar h=\frac{H(x+d)+H(x-d)}2,\qquad
 \nu=\bar h-e,\qquad J=H(x)-\bar h,
 \qquad I=\nu+J=H(x)-e.$$ The average child information is $\nu$, and $J$ is the entropy gained by averaging the two child means. Since $0<d_c<1-x_c\le1$, the mixture has $x\ge0$ and $0<d<1-x$. Entropy concavity gives $\nu\ge0$; strict concavity gives $J>0$; and $e>0$ gives $I<L$. Thus every rate argument lies in $[0,L)$.

<a id="thinning:flag-information"></a>
These quantities have a channel interpretation. Let $X$ be a fair sign and let $B\in\{0,1\}$ be an independent flag with $\Pr(B=1)=\lambda$. Given $X,B$, draw a sign $U$ with conditional mean $$\mathbb E[U\mid X,B]=(1-B)+B(x_c+d_cX).$$ Thus $B=0$ selects the common output and $B=1$ selects the core. The three relevant entropies are $H(U)=H(x)$, $H(U\mid X)=\bar h$, and $H(U\mid X,B)=e$. The chain rule therefore reads $$\label{thinning:flag-information}
 I=I(U;X,B),\qquad \nu=I(U;B\mid X),\qquad
 J=I(U;X),\qquad I=\nu+J.$$ In particular, $\nu$ is the information about the flag after the input has been revealed.

<a id="stmt-thinning-payment"></a>
<div id="thinning:payment" class="theorem">

<a id="thinning:value"></a>
**Theorem 14** (Common-output payment). *For the core and mass in <a href="#thinning:path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:path">[thinning:path]</a>, $$\label{thinning:value}
 F(\nu+J)-F(\nu)\le\lambda F(J_c).$$*

</div>

We prove this by showing that $[F(\nu+J)-F(\nu)]/\lambda$ increases with $\lambda$. This normalization matches the mixture’s cost $\lambda c(a_c,b_c)$. We first compute its derivative and identify the entropy-interval estimate that makes it nonnegative.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The normalized derivative

<a id="thinning:window-data"></a>
For the state <a href="#thinning:path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:path">[thinning:path]</a>, set $$\label{thinning:window-data}
 \begin{gathered}
 p=1+x,\qquad \sigma=1-x,\qquad b=\log(1+x),\\
 q=e+\psi(x)=L-I,\qquad s=e+L-\bar h=L-\nu,\\
 \kappa=-\frac12\log\left(1-\frac{d^2}{p^2}\right)>0.
 \end{gathered}$$ In particular $s-q=J$ and $0<q<s\le L$. Write $$\Delta(\lambda)=F(I)-F(\nu),\qquad
 A=\log\frac2{1+x}=L-b.$$ For $h_{\mathrm{bin}}(t)=H(1-2t)$, the entropy identity is $$t h_{\mathrm{bin}}'(t)-h_{\mathrm{bin}}(t)=\log(1-t).$$ Apply it first to $I=h_{\mathrm{bin}}(\lambda(1-x_c)/2)-\lambda h_c$, then to the two child expressions with initial probabilities $(1-a_c)/2$ and $(1-b_c)/2$, and average. The result is <a id="thinning:path-derivatives"></a>$$\label{thinning:path-derivatives}
 \lambda I'=I-A,\qquad
 \lambda\nu'=\nu-A-\kappa.$$ For the second identity, the two logarithms have average $$\frac12\log\frac{(1+x)^2-d^2}{4}=-A-\kappa.$$

<a id="thinning:normalized-derivative"></a>
Substitution gives the exact Euler derivative $$\label{thinning:normalized-derivative}
 \lambda\Delta'-\Delta
 =\int_\nu^I(u-A)F''(u)\,du+F'(\nu)\kappa.$$ Indeed, at the chosen value of $\lambda$, the derivative in $u$ of $(u-A)F'(u)-F(u)$ is $(u-A)F''(u)$. The value of $A$ is held fixed only during this auxiliary integration; both path derivatives have already been included.

<a id="thinning:derivative-loss"></a>
The last term in <a href="#thinning:normalized-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:normalized-derivative">[thinning:normalized-derivative]</a> is at least $2\kappa$. Convexity makes the integrand nonnegative where $u\ge A$. On the remaining part, the curvature bound $F''(u)\le M/(L-u)^2$ and the substitution $h=L-u$ give $$\label{thinning:derivative-loss}
 \lambda\Delta'-\Delta
 \ge2\kappa-M\int_q^s\frac{(h-b)_+}{h^2}\,dh.$$ Thus it suffices to prove that this integral is at most $3\kappa$: the strict margin $2>3M$ will then make $(\Delta/\lambda)'$ nonnegative. The cutoff $b$ marks exactly the part of the entropy interval where the curvature term can be negative. We will first prove an interval estimate for this kernel, then check its hypotheses using the entropy of the core.

The positive term also has a channel interpretation. For the channel in <a href="#thinning:flag-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:flag-information">[thinning:flag-information]</a>, the posterior of the fair input at the positive output is $$\Pr(X=z\mid U=1)=\frac12\left(1+\frac{zd}{p}\right),
 \qquad z\in\{-1,1\}.$$ Consequently $$\kappa=\frac12\sum_{z=\pm1}
 \log\frac{1/2}{\Pr(X=z\mid U=1)}
 =D_{\mathrm{KL}}\!\left(
 \operatorname{Unif}\{-1,1\}\,\middle\|\,
 \mathcal L(X\mid U=1)\right).$$ This is the relative entropy from the prior to the positive-output posterior, with the flag marginalized. It is an auxiliary path quantity, distinct from the canonical cost $\mathcal K$.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### An estimate for entropy intervals

<a id="stmt-thinning-interval"></a>
<div id="thinning:interval" class="lemma">

<a id="thinning:interval-bound"></a>
**Lemma 15** (An interval estimate with a logarithmic cutoff). *Let $0\le x<1$, and put $$p=1+x,\qquad \sigma=1-x,\qquad
 \gamma=\frac{\sigma}{p},\qquad
 \beta=\frac{\log(1+x)}L.$$ Suppose $$0\le z<\gamma,\qquad \sigma-pz\le u\le1-z.$$ For $\beta>0$, define $k_\beta(v)=(v-\beta)_+/v^2$ for $v>0$ and $k_\beta(0)=0$. For $\beta=0$, set $k_0(v)=1/v$ for $v>0$. Then $$\label{thinning:interval-bound}
 \int_u^{u+z}k_\beta(v)\,dv
 \le-\frac32\log(1-\gamma z).$$ If $x>0$, the endpoint $z=\gamma$ is also allowed. At $z=0$, both sides are zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thinning-interval"><summary>Proof of stmt-thinning-interval</summary>

<a id="thinning:cutoff-bound"></a>
*Proof.* We first bound the cutoff by a rational function: $$\label{thinning:cutoff-bound}
 \beta\ge b_*:=\frac{4x}{3+x},\qquad
 1-\beta\le\frac{3\sigma}{3+x}\le\frac32\gamma.$$ For $0<x\le1$, let $R(x)=(3+x)\log(1+x)/x$. Direct differentiation gives $$x^2R'(x)=N(x):=\frac{x(3+x)}{1+x}-3\log(1+x),
 \qquad N'(x)=-\frac{x(1-x)}{(1+x)^2}\le0.$$ Since $N(0)=0$, $R$ decreases and $R(x)\ge R(1)=4L$. This proves the first inequality, including $x=0$ by continuity. The other two follow from $1-b_*=3\sigma/(3+x)$ and $x\le1$.

<a id="thinning:interval-derivative"></a>
Consider an admissible interval $[u,u+z]$ with $z<\gamma$. The constraints imply $$a:=\frac{2-u}{1+z}\in[1,p].$$ Keep this $a$ and the cutoff $\beta$ fixed, and consider the affine family of intervals <a id="thinning:affine-intervals"></a>$$\label{thinning:affine-intervals}
 u_a(s)=2-a(1+s),\qquad v_a(s)=u_a(s)+s,
 \qquad 0\le s\le z.$$ Because $1\le a\le p$, these intervals satisfy $$0<\sigma-ps\le u_a(s)\le v_a(s)\le1.$$ The initial interval has length zero; the final one is $[u,u+z]$. The Leibniz rule gives $$\frac{d}{ds}\int_{u_a(s)}^{v_a(s)}k_\beta(v)\,dv
 =D_a(s):=a k_\beta(u_a(s))-(a-1)k_\beta(v_a(s)).$$ We claim that $$\label{thinning:interval-derivative}
 D_a(s)\le\frac{3\gamma}{2(1-\gamma s)}.$$

For this estimate, abbreviate $u=u_a(s)$ and $v=v_a(s)$. Above the cutoff, $k_\beta'(v)=(2\beta-v)/v^3$; thus $k_\beta$ first increases and then decreases, with the decreasing case $\beta=0$ included. Its minimum on $[u,1]$ is at an endpoint. Consequently $$k_\beta(v)\ge\min\{k_\beta(u),1-\beta\}.$$ If $k_\beta(u)\le1-\beta$, this gives $D_a\le k_\beta(u)\le1-\beta$, and <a href="#thinning:interval-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:interval-derivative">[thinning:interval-derivative]</a> follows from <a href="#thinning:cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:cutoff-bound">[thinning:cutoff-bound]</a>.

<a id="thinning:polynomial"></a>
Otherwise set $A_*=k_\beta(u)-(1-\beta)>0$. Then $u>\beta$, and $$D_a\le aA_*+(1-\beta),\qquad
 Y:=\frac{1-\gamma s}{\gamma}
 =\frac2\sigma-\frac{2-u}{a}>0.$$ To bound their product, hold $u,\beta,x$ fixed and vary $a$ in the algebraic expression $$\Theta(a)=\{aA_*+(1-\beta)\}
 \left\{\frac2\sigma-\frac{2-u}{a}\right\}.$$ It increases with $a$, since $$\Theta'(a)=\frac{2A_*}{\sigma}
 +\frac{(2-u)(1-\beta)}{a^2}>0.$$ The actual value has $a\le p$, so <a id="thinning:transport-bound"></a>$$\label{thinning:transport-bound}
 YD_a\le\Theta(p)
 =\{p k_\beta(u)-x(1-\beta)\}
   \frac{4x+\sigma u}{\sigma p}.$$ This last expression decreases with $\beta$: its positive factor is independent of $\beta$, and the first factor has derivative $-p/u^2+x<0$. Since $u>\beta\ge b_*$, replacing $\beta$ by $b_*$ keeps $u$ above the cutoff and gives an upper bound. The hard-case condition also gives $$u^2\{k_\beta(u)-(1-\beta)\}
 =(1-u)\{(1-\beta)u-\beta\}>0.$$ Thus $u<1$ and $u>\beta/(1-\beta)\ge b_*/(1-b_*)$. Putting $t=b_*/u$ now yields $0\le t<1/(1+u)$. Using $x=3b_*/(4-b_*)$, substitution and a common denominator give $$\frac32-\left.\Theta(p)\right|_{\beta=b_*}
 =\frac{\mathcal P(t,u)}{2(1-tu)(2+tu)},$$ where the numerator satisfies $$\label{thinning:polynomial}
 \begin{split}
 \mathcal P(t,u):={}&3(2-tu-t^2u^2)\\
 &-\{2(2+tu)(1-t)-3tu^2(1-tu)\}\{1+t(3-u)\}\ge0,
 \qquad 0\le u\le1,\quad 0\le t\le\frac1{1+u}.
 \end{split}$$ To prove this polynomial inequality, put $$C_2=12-8u+8u^2-6u^3,\qquad C_1=3u^2-u-8,\qquad
 C_3=u(3-u)(2-3u^2).$$ Expansion gives <a id="thinning:polynomial-decomposition"></a>$$\label{thinning:polynomial-decomposition}
 \mathcal P=2+C_1t+C_2t^2+C_3t^3.$$ Since $$C_3+u(1+u)=u(1-u)(7+6u-3u^2)\ge0,
 \qquad t(1+u)\le1,$$ we have $C_3t\ge-u$: this is immediate for $C_3\ge0$, while for $C_3<0$ it follows from $C_3\ge-u(1+u)$ and $t\le1/(1+u)$. Hence $\mathcal P\ge2+C_1t+(C_2-u)t^2$. The exact identity $$8(C_2-u)-C_1^2
 =4+(1-u)\{2(5u-3)^2+u^2(1+9u)+10\}\ge4$$ therefore gives $$8\mathcal P\ge(4+C_1t)^2+4t^2>0.$$ This proves <a href="#thinning:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:polynomial">[thinning:polynomial]</a>, including $t=0$. The denominator in the preceding gap is positive because $tu=b_*<1$. Thus $YD_a\le3/2$, which is <a href="#thinning:interval-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:interval-derivative">[thinning:interval-derivative]</a>. Integrating from $0$ to $z$ proves <a href="#thinning:interval-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:interval-bound">[thinning:interval-bound]</a>.

For $x=0$, admissibility forces $a=1$ and the family is $[1-s,1]$, whose lower endpoint is positive for $s<1$. The integrand $k_0(v)=1/v$ therefore causes no endpoint problem. For $x>0$, the cutoff is positive and $k_\beta$ is continuous at zero with the stated extension. Also $\gamma<1$, so continuity along the same family extends the bound to $z=\gamma$. The zero-length case is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The entropy interval of the channel

We now return to the quantities in <a href="#thinning:window-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:window-data">[thinning:window-data]</a>. To apply the interval estimate to $[q,s]$, we need a lower bound on its position and an upper bound on its length $J$. Both follow from the entropy of the oriented core.

<a id="stmt-thinning-window"></a>
<div id="thinning:window" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (The entropy interval on a thinning path). *Every state on <a href="#thinning:path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:path">[thinning:path]</a> satisfies <a id="thinning:window-bound"></a>$$\label{thinning:window-bound}
 \int_q^s\frac{(h-b)_+}{h^2}\,dh\le3\kappa.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thinning-window"><summary>Proof of stmt-thinning-window</summary>

<a id="thinning:quadratic-floor"></a>
*Proof.* The nonnegative center of the core supplies the essential lower entropy bound. Write $h_{\mathrm{bin}}(t)=H(1-2t)$, let $\sigma_c=1-x_c\le1$, and put $$t=\frac{1-d_c/\sigma_c}{2}\in(0,1/2).$$ The two negative-output probabilities at the core are $\sigma_c t$ and $\sigma_c(1-t)$. Concavity and $h_{\mathrm{bin}}(0)=0$ imply $$h_{\mathrm{bin}}(\sigma_c t)\ge\sigma_c h_{\mathrm{bin}}(t),\qquad
 h_{\mathrm{bin}}(\sigma_c(1-t))
 \ge\sigma_c h_{\mathrm{bin}}(1-t)=\sigma_c h_{\mathrm{bin}}(t).$$ Averaging and multiplying by $\lambda$, with $\sigma=\lambda\sigma_c$ and $d/\sigma=d_c/\sigma_c$, gives <a id="thinning:entropy-floor"></a>$$\label{thinning:entropy-floor}
 e\ge\sigma H(d/\sigma).$$ This is a consequence of the oriented common-output path; it is not an assertion about every pair law with the same means. The bound $H(r)\ge L(1-r^2)$ in Lemma <a href="#scalar:entropy-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:entropy-bounds">4</a> now yields $$\label{thinning:quadratic-floor}
 q\ge e\ge L\left(\sigma-\frac{d^2}{\sigma}\right).$$

Expanding the binary entropies in $J$ gives the posterior identity $$J=\frac p2\psi(d/p)+\frac\sigma2\psi(d/\sigma).$$ Using $\psi(r)\le Lr^2$ on both terms, we obtain $$J\le\frac{Ld^2}{\sigma p}=:Lz,\qquad
 0<z<\frac\sigma p.$$ The upper bound on $z$ follows from $d<\sigma$. Together with <a href="#thinning:quadratic-floor" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:quadratic-floor">[thinning:quadratic-floor]</a>, these estimates say $$q\ge L(\sigma-pz),\qquad q+J=s\le L,
 \qquad L(\sigma-pz)+Lz=L(\sigma-xz)\le L.$$ We may therefore enlarge $[q,s]$ to an interval of length $Lz$ inside $[L(\sigma-pz),L]$. More explicitly, choose its lower endpoint as $q_*:=\min\{q,L-Lz\}$. Both candidates are at least $L(\sigma-pz)$, and $q_*\le q$; its upper endpoint is either $q+Lz\ge s$ or $L\ge s$. The integrand is nonnegative, so this enlargement can only increase the integral.

Rescale $h=Lv$. With $\beta=b/L$ the integrand becomes $k_\beta(v)\,dv$, with no additional factor of $L$. The lower endpoint $q_*/L$ and length $z$ satisfy Lemma <a href="#thinning:interval" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thinning:interval">15</a> for this $x$ and $\gamma=\sigma/p$. Hence $$\int_q^s\frac{(h-b)_+}{h^2}\,dh
 \le-\frac32\log(1-\gamma z)
 =-\frac32\log\left(1-\frac{d^2}{p^2}\right)
 =3\kappa.$$ This includes $x=0$, which on the path can occur only at $\lambda=1$, $x_c=0$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-thinning-window-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-thinning-window</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thinning:payment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thinning:payment">14</a>.* Combine the derivative reduction <a href="#thinning:derivative-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:derivative-loss">[thinning:derivative-loss]</a> with Lemma <a href="#thinning:window" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thinning:window">16</a>: $$\begin{aligned}
 \lambda\Delta'-\Delta
 &\ge2\kappa-M\int_q^s\frac{(h-b)_+}{h^2}\,dh\\
 &\ge(2-3M)\kappa\ge0.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus $(\Delta/\lambda)'=(\lambda\Delta'-\Delta)/\lambda^2\ge0$. At $\lambda=1$, the flag is deterministic, so $\nu=0$, $I=J_c$, and $\Delta=F(J_c)$. Comparing with this endpoint proves <a href="#thinning:value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:value">[thinning:value]</a>. No limit as $\lambda$ tends to zero is needed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The canonical high-entropy value

<a id="stmt-thinning-high-value"></a>
<div id="thinning:high-value" class="theorem">

<a id="thinning:high-value-bound"></a>
**Theorem 17** (The canonical high-entropy value comparison). *Let $x\ge0$, $d>0$, and $x+d<1$. Define $$\bar h(x,d)=\frac{H(x+d)+H(x-d)}2,\qquad
 e_0(x,d)=(1-x)H\!\left(\frac d{1-x}\right).$$ For every $e_0(x,d)\le e\le\bar h(x,d)$, $$\label{thinning:high-value-bound}
 \mathcal K(x,d,e)\ge
 J_0(x,d,e):=F(H(x)-e)-F(\bar h(x,d)-e).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thinning-high-value"><summary>Proof of stmt-thinning-high-value</summary>

*Proof.* The canonical classification at <a href="#canonical:transition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:transition">[canonical:transition]</a> represents every state in this closed high-entropy branch by <a href="#thinning:path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:path">[thinning:path]</a>, with a nonnegative core center and $\mathcal K(x,d,e)=\lambda c(a_c,b_c)$. Put $\alpha_c=1-x_c^2$. Lemma <a href="#capacity:onebit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="capacity:onebit">10</a> and convexity of $F$, with $F(0)=0$, give $$c(a_c,b_c)\ge\alpha_c F(J_c/\alpha_c)\ge F(J_c).$$ Theorem <a href="#thinning:payment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thinning:payment">14</a> therefore implies $$J_0(x,d,e)=F(\nu+J)-F(\nu)
 \le\lambda F(J_c)
 \le\lambda c(a_c,b_c)=\mathcal K(x,d,e).$$ ◻

</details>

The canonical optimizer is used here only to evaluate a lower bound on joining cost. For an original pair law with means $x\pm d$ and individual entropies $e_a,e_b$, the value of $e$ in <a href="#thinning:high-value-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="thinning:high-value-bound">[thinning:high-value-bound]</a> is their actual average. The original child information values remain $H(x+d)-e_a$ and $H(x-d)-e_b$; their average is $\bar h(x,d)-e$. Convexity of $F$ therefore gives the common floor at those original child states: $$\frac{F(H(x+d)-e_a)+F(H(x-d)-e_b)}2
 \ge F(\bar h(x,d)-e).$$ No individual entropy moment of the canonical law is substituted for either original child entropy.

<a id="upgrade:section"></a>
### From the linear reserve to the sharp rate

The local reserve inequality does not by itself give the sharp rate $F(I)$ at every state. We now join their maximum $$\widehat{\mathcal B}_m(I)
 =\max\{\mathcal B_m(I),F(I)\}.$$ Closure under a maximum is a useful feature of the local-production framework (Chen, Gohari, and Nair 2025, Lemma 3) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[ChenGohariNair2025](#ref-ChenGohariNair2025)], but $F$ alone does not satisfy a joining inequality for every law. We therefore prove the needed comparison in the part of the domain where the reserve is insufficient. Two comparisons organize the proof. Above half information, the reserve already dominates $F$. Below half information, the demand from $F$ increases with the common mean of the child states. Thus every reflected core can be paid at its one-corner wall by the same thinning theorem used for a one-corner optimizer.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The reserve above half information

<a id="stmt-upgrade-high-information"></a>
<div id="upgrade:high-information" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (The high-information reserve). *For $|m|<1$ and $L/2\le I<H(m)$, <a id="upgrade:high-information-bound"></a>$$\mathcal B_m(I)\ge F(I).
 \label{upgrade:high-information-bound}$$ The inequality is strict if $m\ne0$. Consequently a state with $F(I)>\mathcal B_m(I)$ must have $I<L/2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upgrade-high-information"><summary>Proof of stmt-upgrade-high-information</summary>

<a id="upgrade:centered-curvature"></a>
<a id="upgrade:exponential-balance"></a>
*Proof.* Recall $S(I)=F(I)-2I$ on $[0,L)$ and put $$k_0=L-\frac12,\qquad
 \Xi(I)=(I-k_0)S'(I)-S(I).$$ Since $S(0)=S'(0)=0$ and $S''=F''$ on this interval, $$\Xi(I)=\int_0^I(s-k_0)F''(s)\,ds.
 \label{upgrade:centered-curvature}$$ Lemma <a href="#scalar:curvature-growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:curvature-growth">6</a> says that $w(s)=e^{-2s}F''(s)$ strictly increases. Also $0<k_0<L/2$, and the binary identity $e^L=2$ gives the exact balance $$\int_0^{L/2}(s-k_0)e^{2s}\,ds
 =\left[\frac12e^{2s}(s-L)\right]_0^{L/2}=0.
 \label{upgrade:exponential-balance}$$ On the part of the integral below $k_0$, the factor $s-k_0$ is negative and $w(s)\le w(k_0)$. Above $k_0$, one has $s-k_0>0$ and $w(s)\ge w(k_0)$. Replacing $w(s)$ by $w(k_0)$ therefore gives a lower bound on both parts, strictly so on intervals of positive length. Equations <a href="#upgrade:centered-curvature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upgrade:centered-curvature">[upgrade:centered-curvature]</a>– <a href="#upgrade:exponential-balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upgrade:exponential-balance">[upgrade:exponential-balance]</a> imply $$\Xi(L/2)
 =\int_0^{L/2}(s-k_0)e^{2s}w(s)\,ds>0.$$ Since $\Xi'(I)=(I-k_0)F''(I)>0$ for $I\ge L/2$, we have $\Xi(I)>0$ throughout that range.

For the mean-dependent comparison, set $$\alpha=1-m^2,\qquad
 c_m=H(m)-\alpha L=Lm^2-\psi(m).$$ The elementary entropy bounds give $0\le c_m\le m^2k_0$. Hence, for $I\ge L/2$, the argument $$s=L-\frac{H(m)-I}{\alpha}
   =\frac{I-c_m}{\alpha}$$ is positive; it is less than $L$ because $I<H(m)$. No clipping occurs in this comparison. The tangent inequality for the convex function $S$, and $S'\ge0$, now yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal B_m(I)-F(I)
 &=\alpha S(s)-S(I)\\
 &\ge\alpha[S(I)+(s-I)S'(I)]-S(I)\\
 &=m^2[IS'(I)-S(I)]-c_mS'(I)\\
 &\ge m^2[(I-k_0)S'(I)-S(I)]
 =m^2\Xi(I)\ge0.
\end{aligned}$$ The inequality is strict at a nonzero mean. At mean zero, $\alpha=1$, $c_m=0$, and the two profiles agree identically. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### A direct comparison with the one-corner wall

<a id="stmt-wall-monotonicity"></a>
<div id="wall:monotonicity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (The demand below half information). *Let $m,d>0$, $m+d<1$, and $$0<e\le\bar h(m,d):=\frac{H(m+d)+H(m-d)}2.$$ Put $I=H(m)-e$, $j=\bar h(m,d)-e$, and $J=I-j$. If $I\le L/2$, then at fixed $d,e$, <a id="wall:positive-derivative"></a>$$\partial_m\{F(I)-F(j)\}>0.
 \label{wall:positive-derivative}$$ At $j=0$, the derivative is interpreted from the physical side.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-wall-monotonicity"><summary>Proof of stmt-wall-monotonicity</summary>

<a id="wall:rate-slope"></a>
<a id="wall:rate-ratio"></a>
*Proof.* We compare the rate slopes with the entropy slopes. First, $$\frac{F''(u)}{F'(u)}<2\qquad(0\le u\le L/2).
 \label{wall:rate-slope}$$ Indeed, set $h=L-u\ge L/2$. The lower bound $F''(u)\ge1/[2(L-u)^2]$ from Lemma <a href="#scalar:scaled-curvature" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="scalar:scaled-curvature">7</a> gives $$F'(u)\ge2+\frac12\left(\frac1h-\frac1L\right).$$ Using the upper bound from the same lemma, we get $$2h^2F'(u)\ge(4-L^{-1})h^2+h
 \ge L^2+\frac L4>\frac43L^2\ge h^2F''(u).$$ The quadratic in $h$ increases for $h>0$, and the strict comparison uses $L<3/4$. This proves <a href="#wall:rate-slope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wall:rate-slope">[wall:rate-slope]</a>. Since $0\le j<I\le L/2$, integration gives $$\log\frac{F'(I)}{F'(j)}<2(I-j)=2J.
 \label{wall:rate-ratio}$$

<a id="wall:entropy-ratio"></a>
For the entropy slopes, write $$A=-\partial_m\bar h(m,d)
   =\frac{V(m+d)+V(m-d)}2>0.$$ We claim the reverse comparison $$\log\frac{A}{V(m)}\ge2J.
 \label{wall:entropy-ratio}$$ Hold $m>0$ fixed and set $v_\pm=V(m\pm d)$. The function $G(v)=\cosh^2v-v^2$ is even and strictly increasing for $v>0$, because $G'(v)=\sinh(2v)-2v>0$. Direct differentiation gives $$\partial_d\left\{\log\frac{A}{V(m)}-2J\right\}
 =\frac{G(v_+)-G(v_-)}{v_++v_-}>0.$$ Indeed, $A_d=(\cosh^2v_+-\cosh^2v_-)/2$ and $2J_d=v_+-v_-$. The numerator and denominator are positive since $m+d>|m-d|$, so $v_+>|v_-|$. At $d=0$ the expression in braces is zero. Integration proves <a href="#wall:entropy-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wall:entropy-ratio">[wall:entropy-ratio]</a>.

Finally $I_m=-V(m)$ and $j_m=-A$. Therefore $$\partial_m\{F(I)-F(j)\}
 =-F'(I)V(m)+F'(j)A>0$$ by <a href="#wall:rate-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wall:rate-ratio">[wall:rate-ratio]</a>–<a href="#wall:entropy-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="wall:entropy-ratio">[wall:entropy-ratio]</a>. The regular right derivative $F'(0)=2$ includes a capacity endpoint. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### The local inequality and dimension induction

<a id="stmt-upgrade-joining"></a>
<div id="upgrade:joining" class="theorem">

<a id="upgrade:local-inequality"></a>
**Theorem 20** (Joining the strengthened profile). *Let $(A,B)$ have a joint law on $[-1,1]^2$ with $e_a=\mathbb EH(A)>0$ and $e_b=\mathbb EH(B)>0$. Put $$a=\mathbb EA,\quad b=\mathbb EB,\quad
 m=\frac{a+b}{2},\quad e=\frac{e_a+e_b}{2},\quad
 I=H(m)-e,$$ and $I_a=H(a)-e_a$, $I_b=H(b)-e_b$. Then $$\mathbb Ec(A,B)\ge
 \widehat{\mathcal B}_m(I)
 -\frac{\widehat{\mathcal B}_a(I_a)
             +\widehat{\mathcal B}_b(I_b)}2.
 \label{upgrade:local-inequality}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-upgrade-joining"><summary>Proof of stmt-upgrade-joining</summary>

*Proof.* An infinite joining cost makes the assertion immediate. Otherwise positive child entropies give $|a|,|b|<1$, and entropy concavity makes all the profile arguments physical.

If the parent maximum is attained by $\mathcal B_m(I)$, apply the local reserve inequality of Theorem <a href="#reserve:joining" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="reserve:joining">13</a> and enlarge the two child terms to their $\widehat{\mathcal B}$ values. This includes every tie at the parent.

<a id="upgrade:original-child-pooling"></a>
<a id="upgrade:pure-increment"></a>
It remains to suppose $F(I)>\mathcal B_m(I)$. Put $d=|a-b|/2$ and $$\bar h(m,d)=\frac{H(m+d)+H(m-d)}2,
 \qquad j=\bar h(m,d)-e=\frac{I_a+I_b}{2}.$$ Since $\widehat{\mathcal B}\ge F$, convexity at the original child states gives $$\frac{\widehat{\mathcal B}_a(I_a)
             +\widehat{\mathcal B}_b(I_b)}2
 \ge\frac{F(I_a)+F(I_b)}2\ge F(j).
 \label{upgrade:original-child-pooling}$$ For $d=0$, one has $I=j$ and nonnegativity of cost proves the claim. Otherwise reflect to $m>0$: mean zero is impossible in this strict-parent case, because $\mathcal B_0=F$. Let $$J_0(x,d,e)=F(H(x)-e)-F(\bar h(x,d)-e).$$ The actual law is feasible in the common-budget cost problem, so it is enough to prove $$\mathcal K(m,d,e)\ge J_0(m,d,e).
 \label{upgrade:pure-increment}$$

Use the exact optimizer from Proposition <a href="#canonical:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="canonical:coverage">9</a>. In its one-positive-corner geometry, the entropy is at least the transition value <a href="#canonical:transition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:transition">[canonical:transition]</a>; Theorem <a href="#thinning:high-value" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thinning:high-value">17</a> therefore proves <a href="#upgrade:pure-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upgrade:pure-increment">[upgrade:pure-increment]</a>.

In the reflected geometry <a href="#canonical:reflected" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="canonical:reflected">[canonical:reflected]</a>, write $$d=kr,\qquad e=kH(r),\qquad \ell=1-k,
 \qquad 0<r<1,\quad0<k\le1.$$ Then $0<m\le\ell$, and the cost is $dV(r)$. Hold $d,e,k,r$ fixed and increase the common mean from $m$ to $\ell$. The law with mass $k$ at $(r,-r)$ and masses $(1-k+x)/2$, $(1-k-x)/2$ at the equal corners makes every $x\in[m,\ell]$ physical at the same entropy and cost. Its means remain interior, since $\ell+d=1-k(1-r)<1$.

Lemma <a href="#upgrade:high-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:high-information">18</a> gives $H(m)-e<L/2$. The function $H(x)-e$ decreases along this interval, so Lemma <a href="#wall:monotonicity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="wall:monotonicity">19</a> applies throughout. Consequently $$J_0(m,d,e)\le J_0(\ell,d,e).$$ At $x=\ell$, the negative-corner mass is zero, and the state is at the one-corner transition. Theorem <a href="#thinning:high-value" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thinning:high-value">17</a> gives $$J_0(\ell,d,e)\le dV(r)=\mathcal K(m,d,e).$$ This proves <a href="#upgrade:pure-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upgrade:pure-increment">[upgrade:pure-increment]</a>. A zero-length interval requires only the last comparison. The case $k=1$ would force $m=0$ and is already covered by the reserve branch.

Only the information bound is retained while the mean increases; no claim is made about which profile stays active there. The child terms in <a href="#upgrade:original-child-pooling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upgrade:original-child-pooling">[upgrade:original-child-pooling]</a> always use the original entropies $e_a,e_b$, not the entropies of a replacement optimizer. Together with that pooling inequality, the cost bound completes <a href="#upgrade:local-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="upgrade:local-inequality">[upgrade:local-inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-upgrade-joining-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-upgrade-joining</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#upgrade:production" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:production">3</a>.* Induct on dimension. At dimension zero, a constant field has $D=I=0$ and $\widehat{\mathcal B}_m(0)=0$. For a coordinate split $g_+,g_-$, the exact energy decomposition is $$D(g)=\frac{D(g_+)+D(g_-)}2+\mathbb Ec(g_+,g_-).$$ Each within-face term has its face probability $1/2$; symmetry of the joining cost combines the two orientations of every joining edge into the last expectation. The induction hypothesis bounds the within-face productions, and Theorem <a href="#upgrade:joining" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:joining">20</a> supplies the remaining difference on the joining edges. Their sum is the parent $\widehat{\mathcal B}$ value. Every face is interior-valued, so all child entropies are positive and all local uses are physical. ◻

</details>

<a id="flow:section"></a>
### Sharp entropy interpolation under noise

The sharp production bound has a direct dynamical meaning: a soft field loses information at least as fast as a noisy coordinate with the same information. The appropriate coordinate for this comparison is the inverse entropy deficit, rather than information itself. In that coordinate the comparison flow is simply exponential decay.

<details id="proof-stmt-upgrade-joining-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-upgrade-joining</summary>

<a id="flow:sharp-trajectory"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#main:soft" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:soft">1</a>.* Write $g=u$ for the field in the theorem. First suppose $0<\rho<1$, and write $\rho=e^{-t}$ with $t>0$. If $g$ is constant, both sides of <a href="#main:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:interpolation">[main:interpolation]</a> are zero. Otherwise every value of $g_s=T_{e^{-s}}g$ lies in $(-1,1)$ for $s>0$: the noise kernel gives strictly positive weight to every input, and a nonconstant $[-1,1]$-valued input cannot average to either endpoint. The mean stays fixed. By <a href="#main:flow-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="main:flow-identity">[main:flow-identity]</a> and Theorem <a href="#upgrade:production" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:production">3</a>, $$\frac{d}{ds}I(g_s)=-D(g_s)\le-F(I(g_s))
 \qquad(s>0).
 \label{flow:sharp-trajectory}$$

For a nonconstant input, $g_s$ is nonconstant at every finite time, because the Walsh characters $\chi_A(x)=\prod_{i\in A}x_i$, $A\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}$, form a basis and satisfy $T_{e^{-s}}\chi_A=e^{-s|A|}\chi_A$. All these eigenvalues are positive. Strict convexity of $\psi$ therefore gives $0<I(g_s)<L$. Set $r(s)=\psi^{-1}(I(g_s))\in(0,1)$. The identity $F(\psi(r))=rV(r)$ and $\psi'(r)=V(r)$ turn <a href="#flow:sharp-trajectory" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flow:sharp-trajectory">[flow:sharp-trajectory]</a> into $$V(r(s))r'(s)\le-r(s)V(r(s)),\qquad r'(s)\le-r(s).$$ Thus, for $0<\varepsilon<t$, $$r(t)\le e^{-(t-\varepsilon)}r(\varepsilon).$$ On a finite cube the noise operator and the continuous functional $I$ converge to their initial values as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. Continuity of $\psi^{-1}$, including at $L$, yields $$\psi^{-1}(I(T_{e^{-t}}g))
 \le e^{-t}\psi^{-1}(I(g)).$$ Applying the increasing function $\psi$ proves the claim for $0<\rho<1$. This positive-time argument does not require the initial entropy production to be finite.

At $\rho=0$, the field is constant; at $\rho=1$, the assertion is an identity. For negative correlation, $T_{-\rho}g(x)=T_\rho g(-x)$, and the change of variables $x\mapsto-x$ preserves the uniform measure and information. This proves the full stated range.

For $g(x)=a x_i$ with $|a|\le1$, the initial information is $\psi(|a|)$ and the information after noise is $\psi(|\rho a|)$. Thus every soft coordinate attains equality. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-upgrade-joining-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-upgrade-joining</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#main:ck" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:ck">2</a>.* Since $f$ is Boolean, $H(f(x))=0$ at every input, so $I(f)=H(m)$. Also $I(f(X);Y)=I(T_\rho f)$. The first inequality is therefore Theorem <a href="#main:soft" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:soft">1</a>. The second follows from $r_m\le1$ and monotonicity of $\psi$. If $0<|m|<1$, then $0<H(m)<L$ and $0<r_m<1$, giving the stated strict comparison with the unbiased bound. For a signed coordinate $f$, $T_\rho f=\rho f$, so its information is exactly $\psi(|\rho|)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="flow:comparison"></a>
#### Comparison of mean-dependent production bounds

Theorem <a href="#upgrade:production" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:production">3</a> gives more than the scalar rate needed for Theorem <a href="#main:soft" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:soft">1</a>. For $0<m<1$ and $0\le I<H(m)$, put $h=H(m)-I$. The hybrid production profile of Chen et al. (Chen et al. 2026, sec. 9 and Lemma 9.2) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[ChenEtAl2026CK](#ref-ChenEtAl2026CK)], converted to natural logarithms and the generator $N$ used here, is $$C_m(I)=\max\{F(L-h)-mV(s),F(I)\},\qquad mH(s)=hs,
 \qquad m\le s<1.$$ The equation for $s$ has a unique solution: $H(s)/s$ decreases strictly from $H(m)/m$ to zero on $[m,1)$. In particular, the $F(I)$ branch yields Theorem <a href="#main:soft" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="main:soft">1</a> by the inverse-entropy integration above, with the initial information left unchanged.

<a id="flow:profile-asymptotics"></a>
The mean-dependent estimate in Theorem <a href="#upgrade:production" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="upgrade:production">3</a> has a different strength. At every fixed $m\in(0,1)$, $$\mathcal B_m(H(m)-h)\sim\frac{1-m^2}{2}\log\frac1h,
 \qquad C_m(H(m)-h)\sim\frac{1-m}{2}\log\frac1h
 \quad(h\downarrow0).
 \label{flow:profile-asymptotics}$$ Here is a direct verification. For $r=1-\varepsilon$, $$H(r)=\frac\varepsilon2\log\frac{2\exp(1)}{\varepsilon}
       +O(\varepsilon^2),\qquad
 V(r)=\frac12\log\frac2\varepsilon+O(\varepsilon).$$ Consequently $F(L-h)\sim\tfrac12\log(1/h)$. The equation $H(s)=hs/m$ gives $s\to1$ and $V(s)\sim\tfrac12\log(1/h)$. As $F(H(m)-h)$ remains bounded, these facts give the second equivalent in <a href="#flow:profile-asymptotics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flow:profile-asymptotics">[flow:profile-asymptotics]</a>. The first follows from $$\mathcal B_m(H(m)-h)
 =2(H(m)-h)+(1-m^2)S\left(L-\frac h{1-m^2}\right)$$ and $S(u)=F(u)-2u$ for positive $u$. Thus the displayed profile $C_m$ does not dominate $\mathcal B_m$: the ratio $\mathcal B_m(H(m)-h)/C_m(H(m)-h)$ tends to $1+m>1$ as $h\downarrow0$. This comparison concerns the two explicit production profiles.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Mixture weights for a fixed reflected core $(r,−r)$. The horizontal segment keeps its mass $k$ fixed, so the separation $δ = kr$, entropy $e = kH(r)$, and joining cost $δV(r)$ stay fixed. Moving right increases the mean $m = π_{+} − π_{−}$ until the negative common output disappears. Theorem 20 compares the low-information demand with this endpoint. The triangle is schematic; its points represent probability weights, not channel values.",
      "figure_id": "fig:reflected-core",
      "pdf_page": 11,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:reflected-core",
          "exact_quote": "Mixture weights for a fixed reflected core $(r,-r)$.\nThe horizontal segment keeps its mass $k$ fixed, so the separation\n$\\delta=kr$, entropy $e=kH(r)$, and joining cost $\\delta V(r)$ stay fixed.\nMoving right increases the mean $m=\\pi_+-\\pi_",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.05,>=stealth]\n\\coordinate (N) at (-3,0);\n\\coordinate (P) at (3,0);\n\\coordinate (C) at (0,2.8);\n\\fill[blue!4] (N)--(P)--(C)--cycle;\n\\draw[gray] (N)--(P)--(C)--cycle;\n\\node[below left,align=center] at (N) {$\\pi_-=1$\\\\$(-1,-1)$};\n\\node[below right,align=center] at (P) {$\\pi_+=1$\\\\$(1,1)$};\n\\node[above,align=center] at (C) {$k=1$\\\\$(r,-r)$};\n\\draw[very thick,blue!65!black] (-1.8,1.12)--(1.8,1.12);\n\\draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (-.65,1.34)--(.7,1.34);\n\\node[above] at (0,1.34) {$m$ increases};\n\\fill[blue!65!black] (.35,1.12) circle (2pt);\n\\node[below] at (.35,1.12) {fixed $k$};\n\\fill[blue!65!black] (1.8,1.12) circle (2pt);\n\\node[right,align=left] at (2.35,1.12) {$m=1-k$\\\\$\\pi_-=0$};\n\\node[left] at (-1.92,1.12) {$m=-(1-k)$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "An upright triangle representing mixture weights. The top vertex is labeled \"$k = 1$\" and below it \"$(r, -r)$\". The bottom-left vertex is labeled \"$\\pi_- = 1$\" and below it \"$(-1, -1)$\". The bottom-right vertex is labeled \"$\\pi_+ = 1$\" and below it \"$(1, 1)$\". The left edge of the triangle is labeled \"$m = -(1 - k)$\". The right edge is labeled \"$m = 1 - k$\" and \"$\\pi_- = 0$\". Inside the triangle, a horizontal blue line segment parallel to the base has an arrow pointing to the right, labeled \"$m\\text{ increases}$\" above the segment and \"$\\text{fixed } k$\" below the segment."
    }
  ],
